Dosing Regimen 600 mg daily
Applicant Proposed Treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory acute
Indication(s)/Population(s) myeloid leukemia (AML) with nucleophosmin 1 (NPM1)
mutation
Recommendation on Regular Approval
Regulatory Action
Recommended Treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory acute
Indication(s)/Population(s) myeloid leukemia (AML) with a susceptible nucleophosmin 1
(if applicable) (NPM1) mutation who have no satisfactory alternative
treatment options

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
patients with relapsed or refractory acute myeloid leukemia (AML) with a susceptible
nucleophosmin 1 (NPM1) mutation who have no satisfactory alternative treatment options” using
a dose of 600 mg daily. The recommendation is based on the findings in Study KO-MEN-001 of
a 21.4% CR + CRh rate, median duration of CR + CRh of 5.0 months, and a 21% conversion rate
to transfusion independence (TI). Additional studies are warranted to confirm safety with longterm use, to establish the appropriate dosage in pediatric patients, to establish the appropriate
dosage(s) in patients with organ impairment, and to assess for potential drug interactions.
Development of a companion diagnostic (CDx) to select patients with susceptible NPM1
mutations is recommended.
Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Study KO-MEN-001 (KOMET-001, NCT NCT04067336) was an open-label, Phase 1-2,
multiple cohort, multicenter study of ziftomenib monotherapy in adult patients ≥ 18 years of age
with relapsed or refractory (R/R) acute myeloid leukemia (AML) harboring a mutation in the
NPM1 gene (NPM1m). Patients in the pivotal cohort of the study were required to have NPM1
mutations classified as A, B, or D subtypes; other mutations in NPM1 were permitted if they
were reasonably likely to lead to cytoplasmic localization of the NPM1 protein. Treatment
consisted of ziftomenib at a dose of 600 mg daily until disease progression or unacceptable
toxicity.
The Phase 1 portion was an open-label, dose-escalation study for patients with non-genetically
defined R/R AML; seven single arm cohorts evaluated ziftomenib dosages between 50 and 1000
mg daily (n=30). The Phase 1b portion sought to optimize the dosage of ziftomenib and
compared two dosages (200 mg daily, n=17, and 600 mg daily, n=36) in patients with R/R
NPM1m AML. The Phase 2 portion enrolled 92 patients with R/R NPM1m AML treated at a
ziftomenib dosage of 600 mg daily. Local testing was used for diagnosis and enrollment. The
primary objective of the Phase 2 portion was determination of the CR + CRh rate.
The pivotal cohort was the population of study participants enrolled in the Phase 2 portion of the
study (n=92). This cohort demonstrated a CR + CRh rate of 18.5% (95% confidence interval:
11.1%, 27.9%), with a median DoR of 4.6 months (range 0 to 14.7+ months). Of note, after
adjudication of responses, the lower bound of the CI did not exclude the null hypothesis of 12%.
However, the efficacy data were considered meaningful in a patient population with no
satisfactory alternative treatment options.
Supportive confirmatory data was provided by the Phase 1b portion of KO-MEN-001 (n=20),
which had a CR + CRh rate of 35% (95% CI 15.4, 59.2%), with DoR of 5.6 months (range 1.013.8 months).
Due to the similarities between the Phase 1b and Phase 2 patient populations, the labeled
population includes an analysis of all patients in the Phase 1b and Phase 2 portions of the study
who received treatment with the recommended dosage of ziftomenib. This population included
112 participants with R/R AML. The median number of prior lines of therapy was 2. In this

population, the CR + CRh rate was 21.4%, the median duration of CR + CRh was 5.0 months,
and the rate of conversion to 56-day transfusion independent (TI-56) was 21.2%. The FDA team
noted the low point estimates for CR + CRh and TI-56 rates, along with the short duration of
response. There was a short duration of treatment (median 2.2 months), with only 19% of
participants receiving at least 6 months of ziftomenib therapy. As targeted monotherapy may
require additional time on therapy to achieve response, this limited treatment duration may
underestimate the true response rate in the indicated patient population.
Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence. The relatively low CR + CRh rate,
low TI rate, and short follow-up limiting assessment of durability leads to some uncertainty about
clinical benefit. However, the results from the Phase 2 efficacy cohort of Study KO-MEN-001
and the additional confirmatory data from the Phase 1b portion of KO-MEN-001 are considered
substantial evidence of effectiveness of ziftomenib for the treatment of R/R AML with a
susceptible NPM1 mutation who have no satisfactory alternative treatment options.
Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
R/R AML presents a therapeutic challenge. While curative options exist, these involve the use
of intensive chemotherapy followed by hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT),
which are not available options for older, frailer patients. The median age of diagnosis of
AML is 68 years; at the time of relapse, many patients are not considered eligible for this
intensive, curative therapy. As such, there remains an unmet medical need for patients with
R/R AML. Study KO-MEN-001 was a multiple-cohort dose-escalation and dose-expansion
trial that included 112 participants treated with ziftomenib at the recommended dose of 600
mg daily. In the combined Phase 1b (dose expansion) + Phase 2 (pivotal) cohort, which
comprises the labeled patient population, the CR + CRh rate was 21.4%, the median DoR was
5.0 months, and the TI-56 rate was 21.2%. The review team noted that these low rates
required careful consideration of the overall benefit-risk assessment.
The safety population included the 112 patients in the Phase 1b + Phase 2 cohort treated at the
recommended dosage. The median duration of exposure was 2.2 months (range 0.1-21.5
months); 18% of participants received treatment with ziftomenib for > 6 months.
Fatal adverse reactions (ARs) occurred in 4 (4%) participants, including 2 with fatal
differentiation syndrome (DS), 1 with infection, and 1 with sudden death. Dosage interruption
due to AR occurred in 54% of participants, dose reduction due to an AR in 4% of participants,
and discontinuation of ziftomenib due to an AR in 21% of participants. DS occurred in 26% of
participants, and QTc prolongation occurred in 12% of participants. Median dose intensity
was > 90% in both the Phase 1b and Phase 2 portions of the study, indicating that the
recommended dose of 600 mg daily is tolerable. However, the high rates of DS and high rate
21

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
patients at relapse, many years after initial diagnosis (Chen and Hu, 2020; Cocciardi et al, 2019).
Thus, NPM1-m is a consistent marker of the disease. The mechanism by which mutations in
NPM1 transform healthy hematopoietic cells into leukemia is incompletely understood.
Nonetheless, several studies have demonstrated a critical role of the menin/mixed lineage
leukemia (MLL) (lysine [K]-specific methyltransferase 2A) (KMT2A) complex in
leukemogenesis mediated by NPM1-m (Klossowski et al, 2020; Kühn et al, 2016; Tibsovo®
USPI). Thus, there is a strong rationale for targeting acute leukemia genetic subsets that require
menin-MLL(KMT2A) interaction, such as in NPM1-m AML.
Patients with NPM1-m AML who relapse have a dismal prognosis, with a median aggregate
overall survival (OS) of 6.1 months and a worsening survival outcome with each subsequent line
of therapy. For patients receiving their third line of therapy or greater, median OS is between 5.3
and 3.5 months, respectively (Issa et al, 2023). Notably, these outcomes are seen in patients from
a single large referral center with more than half of the patients receiving multiagent therapy and
a third receiving intensive chemotherapy. Patients in Study KO-MEN-001 were ineligible to
receive these multiagent or intensive chemotherapies. Results from Issa et al, 2023 are a potential
over estimation of the expected outcomes for the proposed intended patient population.
In addition, relapse occurs faster with each subsequent line of therapy as indicated by a
retrospective analysis of 206 NPM1-m AML patients, with median relapse-free survival (RFS)
was as low as 3.3 months in the R/R setting (Issa et al, 2023). While NPM1-m AML may be
associated with a more favorable prognosis compared with other genetic subvariants in newly
diagnosed patients, there was no significant difference in RFS or OS among patients with R/R
NPM1-m AML compared with NPM1 wild-type (NPM1-wt) AML (Issa et al, 2023). This
demonstrates that any positive prognostic value associated with NPM1-m at diagnosis is lost in
the R/R setting.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that the presence of an NPM1 mutation at diagnosis is generally considered to
be a favorable prognostic factor; however, the presence of NPM1 mutation at the time of relapse
does not confer a positive prognostic value. The review team agrees that up to 50% of patients
with AML who achieve remission will later relapse, including those with an NPM1 mutation.
The majority of mutations in the NPM1 gene occur in exon 11 (previously thought to be exon
12) and result in cytoplasmic localization of the NPM1 protein, including the A, B, and D
subtypes. 1 Co-mutations are often identified in analyses of NPM1m AML, including FLT3,

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
The Applicant’s Position:
Therapeutic options are complex and largely rely on the patient’s particular AML genomic
subtype, age at diagnosis, overall performance status, access to standards of care, ability to
tolerate the proposed treatment, and ultimate goals of therapy. Treatment of AML is initially
divided into induction, consolidation, and postremission therapy. Hematopoietic stem cell
transplant (HSCT) is currently the only potentially curative option for AML; however, HSCT is
typically reserved for patients in remission who would clearly benefit from it as it is
accompanied by a distinct set of life-threatening risks and complications (eg, graft-versus-host
disease and higher nonrelapse mortality).
With the goal of eradicating leukemic cells to achieve CR, induction therapy with intensive
chemotherapy is the standard of care for newly diagnosed AML patients able to tolerate the
treatment intensity. In the US, the standard induction chemotherapy (ie, “7+3”) consists of the
combination of continuously infused cytarabine and intermittent dosing of an anthracycline
administered over 7 and 3 days, respectively, and has remained the standard of care for decades.
The combination regimen of venetoclax and azacitidine has received Food and Drug
Administration (FDA) approval for the treatment of patients with newly diagnosed AML who
are ineligible or unfit for standard induction therapy (eg, age and coexisting conditions). Other
treatment options for patients in this category include lower doses of chemotherapy, targeted
agents, or hypomethylating agents.
For consolidation or postremission therapy, treatment consists of HSCT; additional
chemotherapy; and/or targeted therapies, such as FLT3, IDH1, and IDH2 inhibitors, for specific
forms of AML. Per 2023 National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN) guidelines in the
R/R setting, clinical studies are strongly recommended as a treatment option before cycling
through previously used available therapies (Pollyea et al, 2023).
Untreated AML is universally fatal. Currently, there are no FDA-approved or NCCN‑
recommended standard of care treatments for R/R NPM1-m AML and no therapies that target the
underlying disease driver. Despite recent advances in the management of patients with AML,
including the development of targeted and immune therapies and improvements in supportive
care, the OS outcomes for patients with AML remain poor. Considering that NPM1-m AML
represents approximately 30% of AML cases and leads to progressive deterioration in addition to
Othman, J, et al (2024). Molecular, clinical, and therapeutic determinants of outcome in NPM1-mutated AML.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that the above interactions are accurate. In addition, key points of the above and
additional interactions are further described below:
•

EOP1 meeting (2 Feb 2021): FDA recommended having all patients screened by a single
and analytically validated locked test of mutation at a central lab and employing one
test/method using the same analytically validated reagents and procedure at all testing
sites. FDA advised the Sponsor that the acceptable primary endpoint in treatment of R/R
AML without intent to cure is CR + CRh, a direct measure of clinical benefit for
regulatory decision making. In addition, FDA requested justification of proposed null rate
based on historical data. FDA did not object to analyzing the efficacy data independently
for each disease expansion cohort or to aggregating safety information across cohorts to
provide a larger safety dataset for a future NDA submission.

•

Partial Clinical Hold (22 Nov 2021): New enrollment in Study KO-MEN-001 was paused
following a fatal event of differentiation syndrome (DS) in an enrolled participant with a
rearrangement. The partial hold was removed on 20 January 2022 with supplementation
of the DS risk mitigation guidance for investigators.

•

Partial Clinical Hold letter (18 May 2022): Conduct of the healthy volunteer study KOMEN-006 was prohibited following designation of ziftomenib as an epigeneticmodifying agent. The partial hold was removed on 11 January 2023 following
submission of the abbreviated CSR for KO-MEN-006.

•

Type C meeting (28 Nov 2022): FDA agreed that 600 mg QD was a reasonable RP2D for
ziftomenib monotherapy, prompting the initiation of Phase 2 evaluation in adult patients
with R/R AML with NPM1-m. In addition, FDA asked for justification of ziftomenib as a
palliative therapy, for which CRh is considered as a direct clinical benefit. Further
development as monotherapy in patients with KMT2A-r acute leukemia was discontinued.

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
5

Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment:
Ziftomenib is a menin inhibitor. Menin is a nuclear scaffold protein that is part of protein
complexes that regulate gene transcription via chromatin remodeling. Menin binds directly to the
N-terminal of mixed lineage leukemia lysine methyltransferase 2a (MLL [KMT2A]), and this
interaction is involved in nucleophosmin 1(NPM1)-driven gene regulation. Ziftomenib binds to
menin at the KMT2A binding site and disrupts the interaction between menin and KMT2A. The
proposed indication for ziftomenib is for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or
refractory acute myeloid leukemia (AML) with a susceptible NPM1 mutation. The leukemogenic
activity of mutant NPM1 is mediated by the interactions between menin and MLL[KMT2A].
Susceptible NPM1 mutations are defined as those that result in loss of the nucleolar localization
signal and the insertion of a new nuclear export signal leading to the cytoplasmic accumulation
of mutant NPM1 and leukemogenesis.
Ziftomenib inhibited the interaction between recombinant menin and a KMT2A peptide (IC50 =
37 nM), suggesting that ziftomenib can inhibit the interaction between menin and KMT2A
fusion proteins. Ziftomenib showed anti-proliferative activity against a variety of leukemia and
lymphoma cell lines with different KMT2A fusion states (IC50 = 3.9-31 nM). Alterations to
menin-KMT2A target genes were also induced in these models. The in vivo anti-tumor activity
of ziftomenib was evaluated in xenograft models using the human AML cell lines MV4-11 and
MOLM-13 and patient-derived cells in immunocompromised rats and mice with oral
administration and a variety of treatment durations. Overall, ziftomenib showed dose-dependent
anti-tumor activity in these nonclinical in vivo models. These findings are supportive of the
mechanism of action of ziftomenib as described in the label. In binding assays conducted to
determine off-target binding, ziftomenib did not show high levels of activity against the targets
evaluated under the conditions tested, suggesting a low potential for off-target activity in a
physiologically relevant context.
Based on findings from non-GLP and GLP assays, ziftomenib has the potential to block the
human Ether-a-go-go-Related Gene (hERG) and the human L-type voltage-dependent calcium
channel, alpha 1C subunit (hCavl.2) channels. The IC50 values for hERG and hCavl.2 inhibition
were 1.61 and 2.8 μM, respectively. Dose-dependent lower blood pressure and longer PR
intervals were seen after single doses of 40, 120, and 200 mg/kg/day in dogs, while decreases in
heart rate, and increases in RR interval were noted at 200 mg/kg/day during a 13-week repeatdose study in dogs.
The bioavailability of ziftomenib ranged from 11.4% in monkeys to 35% in dogs. Ziftomenib

distribution in the mouse was the highest in the small intestine, stomach wall, cecum, liver, gall
bladder, eye (uveal tract), kidneys, spleen, and Harderian gland at concentrations that were
sustained to 672 hours post-dose. Ziftomenib showed plasma protein binding of greater than 95%
across species and human plasma protein type. In repeat-dose toxicology studies in mice and
dogs, exposure (area under the curve or Cmax) generally increased with increasing dose in a less
than dose proportional manner in 28-day studies and a greater than dose proportional manner in
13-week studies. In vitro studies did not identify unique or major human metabolites.
The GLP-compliant repeat-dose toxicology studies with up to 13 weeks of dosing were
conducted in mice and dogs with animals dosed twice daily via oral gavage. In the 4-week
mouse study, animals received ziftomenib at doses of 40, 120, or 400 mg/kg/day. The high dose
was poorly tolerated, with unscheduled deaths leading to a dosing holiday and a dose reduction
to 200 mg/kg/day. Clinical signs included severe dehydration, decreased activity, and pale skin.
Gross pathology revealed green discoloration in the liver in mid- and high-dose group animals,
which correlated with increased transaminase and bilirubin levels, increased liver weight, and
macrophage infiltration, demonstrating liver toxicity. Microscopic findings of cellular infiltration
and vacuolation were observed in the lung and lymphoid tissues. A decrease in erythrocyte
counts was also observed with associated decreases in hematocrit and hemoglobin.
In the 13-week mouse study, animals were given ziftomenib at doses of 12, 40, or 120
mg/kg/day. Unscheduled deaths occurred in all dosing groups. While the cause of death was not
determined for most of these incidences, the death of a mid-dose group female is notable due to
the malignant lymphoma found in the lymph nodes, thymus, bone marrow, heart, kidney, liver,
lung, ovary, spleen, and stomach. Malignant lymphoma was determined to be the cause of
morbidity. The average exposure to animals at 40 mg/kg/day was approximately 2 times the
exposure in patients receiving the recommended dose of ziftomenib. Given the incidences of
hyperplasia observed with the drug, it is reasonable to conclude that ziftomenib may be
responsible, and the finding has been added to the label. Notable findings for the surviving
animals included reversible basophil, large unstained cell, monocyte, and neutrophil count
increases, irreversible elevated liver enzymes, and increased adrenal gland, pituitary gland,
kidney, liver, spleen, and thymus weights in the high-dose groups. Microscopic findings in the
liver included pigmentation in high-dose groups and dose-dependent hypertrophy among males
and high-dose females. Dose-dependent hyperplasia was observed in the adrenal glands in males
and high-dose females. Ziftomenib exposures at the high dose in mice (AUC = 61500 ng*hr/mL)
were approximately 5 times the human exposure (AUC = 12200 ng*hr/mL) at the recommended
dose of 600 mg once daily.
In the 4-week dog study, ziftomenib was administered at doses of 20, 60, or 200 mg/kg/day. Two
unscheduled deaths occurred in high-dose group males that were likely due to severe alveolar
edema and hemorrhage. Surviving mid-dose and high-dose animals showed clinical findings of
dehydration, abnormal feces, and vomitus that corelated with gastrointestinal tract toxicity noted

vacuolation, and lymphoid depletion was noted microscopically. The microscopic findings
persisted at the end of the recovery period.
In the 13-week toxicology study in dogs, animals were given ziftomenib at doses of 6, 20, or 60
mg/kg/day. While all animals survived to the scheduled necropsies, clinical findings included
reversible reduced activity, hunched posture, wet fur, salivation, and thin appearance in the highdose group animals. Electrocardiogram readings identified a decrease in heart rate that correlated
with increased RR intervals. The liver was the primary organ of toxicity, where elevated liver
enzyme levels in the blood were observed along with discoloring and microscopic findings of
cholestasis and biliary hyperplasia. Other organs of toxicity included the lymph node, spleen,
gall bladder, thymus, ovaries, bone marrow, adrenal glands, parathyroid gland (females),
pituitary gland, and epididymis (reduced sperm). A high-dose group female had findings of
hyperplasia in several tissues, including the liver, pancreas, thymus, heart, aorta, adrenal glands,
and mammary glands. The observance of hyperplasia in both mice and dogs is being added to
Section 13.2 of the label. Ziftomenib exposures at the high dose in dogs (AUC = 18700
ng*hr/mL) were approximately 1.5 times the human exposure (12200 ng*hr/mL) at the
recommended dose of 600 mg once daily. Clinical adverse events noted in the label include
infection, differentiation syndrome, febrile neutropenia, pyrexia, electrocardiogram QT
prolongation, bacterial infection, leukocytosis, cardiac failure, cholecystitis, diarrhea, and
pruritus. There is overlap between the toxicology profile in the dog, with the immune and cardiac
systems as affected areas, and the noted clinical observations.
In the pivotal embryo-fetal development study, pregnant mice received ziftomenib doses of 6,
20, or 60 mg/kg/day during organogenesis from gestational day 6 to gestational day 15. Maternal
body weight and weight gains were lower in the high-dose animals by the end of the dosing
period and continued to the end of the recovery period. Post-implantation loss was higher in the
high-dose group due to a higher number of dead fetuses, which in turn was due to 1 high-dose
group animal that had a litter entirely composed of dead fetuses. Mean fetal weights were
modestly lower in all test article groups. External malformations were elevated in the high-dose
group fetuses and composed of cleft palates. Skeletal malformations were observed in all test
article dose groups, notably in the ribs, skull, and vertebra. Exposures at the lowest dose were
approximately 0.3 times the human exposure (AUC) at the recommended clinical dose of 600
mg once daily. Based on data from the embryo-fetal development study and ziftomenib’s
mechanism of action, a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity is included in the labeling for this
product. The 13-week toxicity study in dogs showed changes to male and female reproductive
organs that included decreased testis weight, increased vacuolation of the seminiferous tubular
epithelial cells, a marked decrease in sperm in the epididymal lumen, and fewer developing
follicles in the ovaries. At the dose of 60 mg/kg/day, with exposures as low as 1.2 times the

Pharmacology
Primary Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
In vitro studies demonstrated that ziftomenib inhibited binding of an MLL(KMT2A)-derived
peptide to menin in a cell-free biochemical assay with an IC50 of 37 nM (Study KO-PD-0015)
and inhibited cell proliferation with IC50 values ranging from 4 to 31 nM in a panel of 6
KMT2A-r cell lines (Study KO-PD-0016). Ziftomenib showed no activity, with IC50 values ≥
1800 nM, in 4 menin-independent AML cell lines. Ziftomenib induced apoptosis in KMT2A-r
cell lines in a time- and concentration-dependent manner, peaking at around 80% in MV4;11
cells and 60% in KOPN8 cells after 7 days of treatment (Study KO-PD-0017). Ziftomenib drove
changes in menin-dependent gene expression that were detectable within 2 to 4 days and that
increased up to Day 7 (Study KO-PD-0018) including inhibiting expression of HOXA9 and
MEIS1 with IC50 values around 10 nM and 50 nM cells after 4 days of treatment in the KMT2Ar cell lines MV4;11 and KOPN8, respectively; inhibiting expression of MEIS1 with an IC50 of
6.6 nM in the KMT2A-wt, NPM1/DNMT3A-mutant cell line OCI-AML3 and inducing
expression of the differentiation marker CD11b in all 3 cell lines with an IC50 of approximately
50 nM.

In vitro evaluation of the metabolite KO-516 and metabolite KO-739
KO-516 and KO-739 are pharmacologically active metabolites and were identified in mouse and
dog plama as main metabolites. They are also present in human plama as minor metabolites.
KO-516 inhibited binding of an MLL(KMT2A)-derived peptide to menin in a cell-free
biochemical assay with an IC50 of 27 ± 13 nM (Study KO-PD-0015) and inhibited cell
proliferation with IC50 values ranging from to 4 to 24 nM for 4 KMT2A-r cell lines (Study KOPD-0016).
KO-739 inhibited menin-MLL(KMT2A) peptide interactions with IC50 values of 48.56 nM and
52.50 nM for 2 separate batches of menin (Study KO-PD-0201) and inhibited cell proliferation
with an IC50 value of 44.1 ± 14.8 nM in the KMT2A-r cell line MV4;11 (Study KO-PD-0200).
In Vivo Cell Line-Derived Xenograft (CDX) Models
SC and disseminated MV4;11 CDX rodent models of KMT2A-r AML:
•

In the disseminated MV4;11 mouse model, ziftomenib induced stasis at 50 mg/kg/day
and regression at 100 mg/kg/day following once daily (QD) treatment for 6 days (Study
KO-PD-0019). Therapeutic activity was accompanied by dose-dependent downregulation
of the menin-MLL(KMT2A) target genes HOXA9 (~2-fold.) and MEIS1 (almost 50fold) as well as induction of CD11b (~25-fold) in bone marrow.

•

In the SC MV4;11 mouse model, ziftomenib at 50 or 100 mg/kg/day reduced tumor
burden to undetectable levels within 2 to 4 weeks following QD oral (PO) administration
(Study KO-PD-0020). All ziftomenib-treated animals remained tumor free following a
33-day post-treatment observation period.

•

In the disseminated MV4;11 mouse model, ziftomenib at 100 mg/kg/day reduced
circulating blasts to trace levels within 16 days of the 3-week dosing period (Study KOPD-0021). Mice treated at 50 or 100 mg/kg/day ziftomenib survived at least 24 days
longer than controls although all animals eventually succumbed.

•

In the SC MV4;11 nude rat model, ziftomenib at 30 or 100 mg/kg/day for 28 days led to
100% tumor growth inhibition (TGI) in all animals (Study KO-PD-0024).

Hyper-aggressive disseminated MOLM13 CDX mouse model of KMT2A-r AML:
•

Ziftomenib dosed at 100 mg/kg/day QD for 29 days prevented leukemia progression
during the dosing period (Study KO-PD-0022). Animals relapsed in a dose-dependent
fashion, with median survival prolonged from 17 days in the vehicle group to 20, 26, and
48 days in mice treated with ziftomenib at 25, 50, and 100 mg/kg/day, respectively.

•

When ziftomenib was tested in comparison to and in combination with the standard-ofcare treatments azacitidine and venetoclax, ziftomenib at 100 mg/kg/day QD for 42 days
beginning 3 days post-transplantation prolonged median survival time beyond the 96-day

In Vivo Patient-Derived Xenograft (PDX) Models
Disseminated PDX mouse model of KMT2A-r AML bearing an MLL(KMT2A)-P300

•

125-day follow-up period as compared to a median survival of 42 days in the control
animals.

Disseminated AM7577 NPM1/DNMT3A/IDH2/FLT3-mutant PDX mouse model of KMT2A-wt
AML in comparison to and in combination with the FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 (FLT3)-inhibitor
quizartinib (AC220) (Study KO-PD-0025):
•

Ziftomenib at 100 or 150 mg/kg/day alone or at 100 mg/kg/day in combination with
AC220 at 2 mg/kg/day administered QD for 6 weeks resulted in no detectable human
leukemic blasts at sacrifice whereas all vehicle-treated and most AC220-treated animals
were inundated with blasts at sacrifice. No animals treated with ziftomenib, alone or in
combination with AC220, succumbed to leukemia-related disease during the 100-day
experiment, while AC220 as a single agent prolonged survival of tumor-bearing mice by
193% relative to controls

Ziftomenib tested with several intermittent dosing schedules that introduced dosing holidays
after 1, 2, or 3 weeks of treatment in the disseminated AM7577 NPM1/DNMT3A/IDH2/FLT3mutant PDX mouse model of KMT2A-wt AML (Study KO-PD-0205) showed that
•

Ziftomenib at 50 mg/kg/day for Weeks 1 to 8 or at 100 mg/kg/day for Weeks 1 to 8;
Weeks 1 to 3 and 4 to 7; Weeks 1 to 2 and 4 to 6; or Weeks 1, 3, 5, and 7 reduced
circulating blast counts below detectable levels within 28 days of the 56-day dosing
period

•

All ziftomenib-treated animals survived and remained tumor free at the end of the
experiment (following a further 8-week observation period) with the exception of 2 mice
treated with the 1-week-on/1-week-off regimen

•

The minimum fully efficacious dose of ziftomenib on a continuous schedule in this
model was 50 mg/kg QD, and the minimum fully efficacious continuous dosing period
was 2 weeks.

In vivo evaluation of antitumor activity of ziftomenib tested in comparison to AC220 in the
LEXFAM 2734 NPM1/IDH1/FLT3-mutant PDX model of KMT2A-wt AML in NOG mice
(Study KO-PD-0026) found that
•

Ziftomenib at 150 mg/kg/day QD for 61 days resulted in reduced tumor load at
termination to 0.44% of peripheral blood cells as compared to 45.7% and 61.6% for
AC220- and vehicle-treated animals, respectively.

•

Tumor load at termination in bone marrow was 18.8%, 53.2%, and 31.6% of cells for
ziftomenib-, AC220-, and vehicle-treated groups, respectively, with 4 of 5 ziftomenibtreated animals exhibiting almost undetectable levels of tumor cells.

•

Human cells in the ziftomenib-treated mice showed clear evidence of myeloid
differentiation, with 77.3% of human cells expressing CD11b at Day 35

Ziftomenib-treated animals had 60% survival at Day 76 as compared to 0% survival by Day 76
in the AC220-treated animals and 0% survival by Day 37 in control animals.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA concurs with the Applicant’s assessment for the primary pharmacology of ziftomenib.
By disrupting the interactions between menin and KMT2A (wild-type or rearranged), menin
inhibitors disrupt the leukemogenic transcriptional signaling induced by chromatin-binding
complexes that are facilitated by and contain menin and rearranged KMT2A. NPM1 is a
multifunctional protein that typically resides in the nucleolus but becomes mainly cytosolic and a
leukemogenic driver when it is mutated (NPM1c). The leukemogenic activity of NPM1c is
mediated by the activity of the menin/KMT2A (wild-type or rearranged) chromatin-modulating
complex and is inhibited by the activity of menin inhibitors.

ADME/PK
The Applicant’s Position:
Absorption
In Vitro
Ziftomenib had moderate permeability in human colorectal adenocarcinoma (Caco-2) cell
monolayers (Study KO-ADME-0037).
Ziftomenib transporter substrate studies (Studies KO-ADME-0038, KO-ADME-0200):
Transporter substrate assessments demonstrated that ziftomenib was a breast cancer resistance
protein (BCRP) substrate and a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate in Madin-Darby canine kidney
II (MDCKII) cells. Ziftomenib was not a bile salt export pump (BSEP) or multidrug
resistance-associated protein 2 (MRP2) substrate.
Transporter inhibition studies (Study KO-ADME-0038): Transporter inhibition assessments
demonstrated that ziftomenib was not an inhibitor of BCRP, organic anion transporter
(OAT)1, OAT3, organic anion transporting polypeptide (OCT)2, or multidrug and toxin
extrusion transporter (MATE)2-K under the conditions of this study, and ziftomenib had no
meaningful inhibitory effect on the remaining transporters tested as follows: Ziftomenib was
not an inhibitor of P-gp (inhibited no more than 29.0% of uptake at 5 μM, the highest
concentration tested), organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP) 1B1 (inhibited no more
than 18.8% of uptake at 5 μM, the highest concentration tested), or OATP1B3 (inhibited no
more than 13.1% of uptake at 5 μM, the highest concentration tested), as the inhibition was
less than 50%. Ziftomenib was not an inhibitor of BSEP, MRP2, and MATE1-mediated uptake
at the highest concentrations tested, as inhibition was less than 5%.
In Vivo
Single-Dose Pharmacokinetics across species of an Intravenous Bolus or Oral Dose of
ziftomenib (Studies KO-ADME-0032, KO-ADME-0033, KO-ADME-0034, KO-ADME0035)
Orally administered 10 mg/kg, ziftomenib was rapidly absorbed in female mice (time to
maximum concentration [tmax] of 1.0 h), rats (4.7 h), dogs (2.7 h), and monkeys (4.0 h). The
oral bioavailability of ziftomenib was 34% in BALB/c mice, 24% in Sprague Dawley rats,
35% in beagle dogs, and 11% in cynomolgus monkeys. Plasma clearance (CL) was low in
mice and moderate in rats, dogs, and monkeys. The volume of distribution at steady state (Vss)
was low in mice and high in rats, dogs, and monkeys. The elimination half-life (t1/2) was 3.5 h
in mice, 10 h in rats, 11.6 h in dogs and 9.1 h in monkeys.
Toxicokinetics in GLP compliant 28 day and 13-week oral repeat dose toxicity studies in
CD-1 mice (Studies KO-TOX-0009, KO-TOX-0010) and beagle dogs (Studies KO-TOX0012, KO-TOX-0013)

Pharmacokinetics, Distribution, Metabolism, and Excretion of 14C-KO-539 Following
Oral Administration to Mice / Study KO-ADME-0044 / Section 4.2.1 of Module 2.6.4,
Section 6 of Module 2.6.5
After single oral gavage administration of [14C]ziftomenib in BALB/c and C57BL/6 mice,
radioactivity was distributed to tissues, with most tissues having reached Cmax by 24 h post
dose. Distribution trends were similar across pigmented and nonpigmented strains, except that
radioactivity was distributed in the uveal tract, indicating binding to melanin-containing
tissues with evidence of elimination of radioactivity from melanin containing tissues over
time. Low levels of radioactivity were detected in the brain at 24 h, but these were the lowest
Cmax values observed of all tissues, indicating a low extent of [14C]ziftomenib-derived
radioactivity crossing the blood-brain barrier. Low levels of radioactivity were measured in the
testis, also indicating the low extent of [14C]ziftomenib-derived radioactivity crossed the
blood-testis barrier.
Metabolism
In Vitro
Evaluation of the In Vitro Metabolic Stability of KO-539 Using Liver Microsomes of
Various Mammalian Species / Study KO-ADME-0047 / Section 5.1.1.1 of Module 2.6.4,
Section 10 of Module 2.6.5
Metabolite Profiling of Ziftomenib in Liver Microsomes Across Species / Study KOADME-0049 / Section 5.1.1.2 of Module 2.6.4, Section 10 of Module 2.6.5
The ziftomenib in vitro t1/2 values in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human liver microsomes
were 22.9, 21.6, 55, 8.4, and 19.4 minutes, respectively. The short t1/2 in monkey is indicative
of extensive first pass effect and in agreement with low bioavailability in monkey.
In vitro metabolism in liver microsomes from the toxicological test species, mouse and dog,
and humans was similar and there were no unique human metabolites. The N-demethylated
metabolite M1 (KO-516) and metabolite M9 (KO-739) were identified as the major
metabolites in mouse, dog, and human liver microsomal incubations. However, the human
AME study demonstrated that both metabolites were minor, accounting for <10% of total
drug-related exposure, thus not requiring further evaluation of the metabolites.

Identification of KO-539 In Vitro and In Vivo Metabolites / Study KO-ADME-0049 /
Section 5.2.1 of Module 2.6.4, Section 11 of Module 2.6.5
After single oral dosing of ziftomenib to MV4;11-tumor-bearing nude mice, KO-516
represented a major circulating metabolite in mouse with 35% area AUClast exposure of
ziftomenib in plasma. When in vivo metabolism of ziftomenib was evaluated in excreta from
CD-1 mice after single IV administration of 50 mg/kg unlabeled ziftomenib, metabolites KO516 and KO-739 were found to be greater than 10% of the sum of peak areas in plasma and
feces.
Evaluation of the in vivo metabolism in plasma after single oral administration of
[14C]ziftomenib to male and female BALB/c mice showed that the major circulating
radioactive component was unchanged ziftomenib, accounting for 19.4% and 8.09% of the
total radioactivity in AUC in male and female mice plasma, respectively. No single metabolite
represented >10% of the total radioactivity. Of the 5 metabolites that were identified, KO-739
(M9) was the abundant circulating metabolite and accounted for 8.47% of total radioactivity
exposure in male mice, and KO-739 (M9) was not identified in plasma from female mice. The
KO-516/N-[des-N-methylsulfoxide-pyrazinyl]-oxy-KO-516 (M1/M6) mixture was an
abundant component in males and females and accounted for 6.30% and 5.77% of total
radioactivity exposure, respectively. These data are in agreement with the human AME data,
which demonstrated that all metabolites were minor (<10% of total drug-related exposure).
In feces, unchanged ziftomenib accounted for 56.8% and 51.1% of the total dose in male and
female mouse, respectively. Of the minor metabolites identified, KO-739 (M9) was the most
abundant, accounting for 9.47% and 9.24% of the dose in males and females, respectively.
KO-516 (M1) accounted for 4.83% and 5.77% in fecal samples from males and females,
respectively. In urine, trace levels of ziftomenib and metabolites were excreted, representing
1.35% and 1.60% of administered radioactivity in males and females, respectively.
CYP Enzyme Reaction Phenotyping for ziftomenib using human hepatocytes, human
recombinant CYP enzyme isoforms, and human liver microsomes / Studies KO-ADME0048, KO- ADME-0050, and KO-ADME-0056 / Section 5.1.1.4 of Module 2.6.4, Section
10 of Module 2.6.5
All studies demonstrated that ziftomenib was metabolized by cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A4, as
the predominant isoform, and to a lesser extent by CYP1A2, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6, and CYP2J2. PBPK modeling using clinical data demonstrated that there were no
clinically relevant DDIs between ziftomenib and strong CYP3A4 inhibitors such as
itraconazole. This is described in Section 8.

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
In the first study, direct inhibition by ziftomenib (0.05 to 50 µM) was observed for CYP3A4
(IC50 12.2 μM; midazolam as substrate), CYP2C19 (IC50 11.2 μM), CYP2C9 (IC50 9.48 μM),
and CYP2C8 (IC50 20.6 μM). Preincubation of ziftomenib with human liver microsomes for 30
minutes prior to addition of the NADPH-regenerating system shifted the IC50 value to 2.86 μM
for CYP3A4 (midazolam) inhibition, suggesting a mild propensity for TDI of CYP3A4. In the
second study, ziftomenib at lower concentrations (0.005 to 5 μM) directly inhibited CYP3A4/5
activity (midazolam 1′-hydroxylation) with an IC50 value of 1.9 μM. Ziftomenib was a
metabolism-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A4/5; IC50 values shifted from 1.9 μM to 0.22 μM
(midazolam) and >5 μM to 0.44 μM (testosterone). The R values suggest that a clinically
relevant DDI is possible following co- administration of a CYP3A4 substrate. However, PBPK
modeling using clinical data demonstrated that there were no DDIs between ziftomenib and
CYP3A4 substrates such as midazolam..
UGT Inhibition by ziftomenib in Human Liver Microsomes / Study KO-ADME-0061 /
Section 5.1.1.6 of Module 2.6.4, Section 13 of Module 2.6.5
Ziftomenib (0.0225 to 22.5 μM) was incubated (37°C) with microsomes, cofactor, and probe
substrates specific for UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9, UGT2B7,
UGT2B15, or UGT2B17. Samples were analyzed by LC-MS/MS. Ziftomenib directly
inhibited UGT1A1, UGT1A9, and UGT2B17 with IC50 values of 3.4, 1.4, and 1.1 μM,
respectively. At the clinical dose of 600 mg once daily, unbound ziftomenib mean Cmax at
steady state (Cmax,ss) is expected to be approximately 0.1 μM, which is significantly lower than
the IC50 of UGT inhibition. Thus, these in vitro data are not expected to be clinically relevant.
CYP Enzyme Induction by ziftomenib / Studies KO-ADME-0059, KO-ADME-0066 /
Section 5.1.1.7 of Module 2.6.4, Section 13 of Module 2.6.5
Ziftomenib resulted in a 2.19- to 2.94-fold induction in CYP1A2 mRNA across the 3 donors
tested, compared to >36-fold inductions with the positive control (Study KO-ADME-0066). In
a subsequent analysis using a broad concentration range, ziftomenib resulted in concentrationdependent increases in CYP1A2 mRNA up to 4.86-fold with maximal fold induction (Emax)
values ranging from 1.87- to 2.86-fold change and 50% maximal effect (EC50) values of
induction ranging from 0.976 to 2.82 μM (Study KO-ADME-0059). In both studies,
ziftomenib was considered an in vitro inducer of CYP1A2 but not CYP2B6 or CYP3A4. At
the dose of 600 mg once daily, unbound ziftomenib mean Cmax,ss is expected to be
approximately 0.1 μM, which is significantly lower than the EC50 of CYP1A2 induction. Thus,
these in vitro data are not expected to be clinically relevant.

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
Urinary and Fecal Excretion of KO-539 and Its Major Metabolite M1 (KO-516) in Mice
After an Intravenous Bolus Dose / KO-ADME-0063 / Section 6.2 of Module 2.6.4, Section
14 of Module 2.6.5
Based on a study with oral administration of [14C]ziftomenib in BALB/c mice, radioactivity
was mainly excreted via the fecal route. At 168 h postdose, approximately 86.0% of the total
dose was recovered in feces in males and females. Urinary excretion played a minor role, and
approximately 1.5% of the dose was recovered in urine in males and females.
In excreta from CD-1 mice after single IV bolus administration of 25 and 50 mg/kg ziftomenib
approximately 2.4% and 2.8% of unchanged ziftomenib was excreted through bile into feces
during 48 h postdose, after 25 and 50 mg/kg dosing, respectively. Biliary excretion of KO-516
comprised approximately 2.4% of the administered ziftomenib dose at both dose levels tested.
Approximately 0.04% and 0.25% of unchanged ziftomenib was excreted through urine during
the first 48 h following a single IV administration at 25 and 50 mg/kg, respectively, whereas
renally excreted KO-516 comprised approximately 0.01% of the administered ziftomenib dose
at both dose levels.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. See Sections 6 and 14 for FDA’s
assessment of drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies.
Toxicokinetics
In the 7-day repeat-dose toxicology studies in mice and dogs, exposure (Cmax and AUC)
increased with increasing dose, but in a manner that was less than dose-proportional. In the 28day repeat-dose toxicology studies in mice and dogs, exposure increased with increasing dose in
a manner that was dose-proportional or less than dose-proportional. In the 13-week repeat-dose
toxicology studies in mice and dogs, exposure increased in a manner that was dose-proportional
in mice but greater than dose-proportional in dogs with increasing dose. Exposure was similar in
males and females for the 7-day, 28-day, and 13-week studies.

NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
Toxicology
General Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Ziftomenib is a menin inhibitor intended for the treatment of advance hematologic malignancies.
The ziftomenib toxicology program was designed in accordance with ICH S9 guidance and other
relevant ICH guidances on safety. All pivotal studies were conducted in accordance with GLP
and currently accepted guidelines. Ziftomenib was tested in a single-dose study in mice and
exploratory 7-day dose-range finding studies in mice and dogs. The definitive nonclinical safety
program was comprised of 28-day and 13-week toxicity studies in mice and dogs, as well as the
standard battery of genetic toxicology studies, in vitro and in vivo phototoxicity studies, and an
embryofetal development study in mice.
Pivotal Repeat Dose Toxicity Studies
28-Day Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of KO-539 in Mice with a 28-Day
Recovery Period / Study No. KO-TOX-0009 / Section 3.2 of Module 2.6.6, Section 7.1.1 of
Module 2.6.7
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
•

•

Dose limiting toxicities (adverse body weight loss and dehydration) were observed at 400
mg/kg/daythe high dose was adjusted to 200 mg/kg/day (Day 12/14). Unscheduled deaths
(Day 17 and Day 22) were due to body weight loss and dehydration and likely contribution
of histopathologically, ziftomenib-related microscopic findings in the liver, kidney, lung,
spleen, and lymph nodes.
In scheduled sacrificed animals adverse microscopic findings at the terminal necropsy
were noted in the liver, lung, kidney, spleen, and lymph nodes (mandibular, axillary, and
mesenteric).

GLP compliance: Yes
Methods

dosing:

0 mg/kg bid (0 mg/kg/day), 20 mg/kg bid (40 mg/kg/day), 60
mg/kg bid (120 mg/kg/day), and 200/100 mg/kg bid (400/200
mg/kg/day) for 28 days. Daily doses were administered at
least 6 hours, but not more than 7 hours, apart.
Dose-limiting toxicity was observed at; 400 mg/kg/day after
approximately 1 week of dosing. Adverse body weight loss
(in some instances, greater than -15% on Day 8 vs Day 1) and
dehydration were noted. Beginning on Day 9/11 high-dose

Observations and Results:
Parameters

Major findings

Three unscheduled deaths in males from the high-dose group were considered
related to ziftomenib administration. These males were euthanized in extremis
(2 on Day 17 and 1 on Day 22) due to adverse body weight loss and
worsening clinical condition. Clinical observations generally noted for these
animals prior to termination included suspected dehydration, unkempt
Mortality
appearance, hunched posture, decreased feces, pale skin (generalized),
decreased activity, and/or cold to touch. On the day of euthanasia, body
weight values for these males was decreased (-27% to -33%) versus respective
Day 1 values. Although a definitive cause of moribundity could not be
determined histopathologically, ziftomenib-related microscopic findings in the
liver, kidney, lung, spleen, and lymph nodes were considered contributory.
At scheduled termination, ziftomenib-related clinical observations were
limited to generally low frequencies of delayed skin turgor and eyes partly
Clinical signs closed among high-dose males and/or females. Additionally, limited
occurrences of dehydration were noted for high-dose males during veterinary
examination.
Beginning on Day 15 and continuing through the end of the recovery period,
mean body weight values for high-dose males and females were generally
Body weight
comparable to, and occasionally greater than, the respective concurrent control
values.

As evidence of bilirubin staining (characterized by the presence of green
material using light microscopy) was not observed for any animal at either
necropsy interval, the granular eosinophilic/golden material within
centrilobular hepatocytes was not interpreted to be bile and/or bile-containing
material.
Transmission electron microscopy (TEM) revealed an increased number of
and/or larger lysosomes containing phospholipid material (in lamellar,
concentric, and/or myelinoid aggregates, with rare multivesiculate bodies)
within the cytoplasm of pulmonary alveolar macrophages of high-dose
terminal necropsy animals when compared with lysosomes of vehicle-treated
Other
concurrent control animals. The increased incidence and/or severity of
relevant
accumulation of lysosomal phospholipid material in alveolar macrophages was
evaluations
consistent with a diagnosis of phospholipidosis.
Also within the high-dose recovery animals evaluated, increased numbers of
and/or larger lysosomes containing phospholipid material were observed
within the cytoplasm of pulmonary alveolar macrophages, consistent with
phospholipidosis. However, these lysosomes were less prominent and
numerous than those observed in high-dose animals at the terminal necropsy,
suggesting a lack of nascently retained phospholipid material. Additionally,
the presence of more numerous telolysosomes (lysosomes having accumulated
indigestible/end-stage material such as lipofuscin) in high-dose recovery
animals was considered to represent the progression of lysosomal digestion
Systemic exposure to ziftomenib and KO-516 (metabolite) was independent of
sex. Cmax values for ziftomenib and KO-516 increased with increasing dose in
a less-than-dose-proportional manner on Days 1 and 29. AUC0-24h values for
ziftomenib and KO-516 increased with increasing dose in an approximately
dose-proportional manner from 40 to 120 mg/kg/day and in a less-than- doseToxicokinetic
proportional manner from 40 to 400 mg/kg/day on Day 1. On Day 29, AUC0s
24h values for ziftomenib and KO-516 increased with increasing dose in an
approximately dose-proportional manner from 40 to 200 mg/kg/day. Systemic
exposure to ziftomenib and KO-516 did not change following repeated bid
administration of ziftomenib. Systemic exposure (AUC0-24h) to KO-516 was
approximately 23% to 33% of the systemic exposure of ziftomenib.

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
A 28-Day Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of KO-539 in Beagle Dogs with
a 28-Day Recovery Period/ Study No. KO-TOX-0012 / Section 3.5 of Module 2.6.6,
Section 7.2.1 of Module 2.6.7
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings
•

Microscopic findings that were considered adverse (at 200 mg/kg/day) included
basophilic accumulations and increased cellularity of basophilic macrophages
involving the liver, bile duct, gallbladder, and intestines; increased cellularity of
vacuolated macrophages within the lymphoid organs, lymphoid depletion of the
thymus, Peyer’s patches, and lymph nodes, and membranous glomerulopathy. Findings
persisted at the end of the recovery period.

GLP compliance: Yes
Methods
Dose and frequency of
dosing:

0 mg/kg bid (0 mg/kg/day), 10 mg/kg bid (20 mg/kg/day), 30
mg/kg bid (60 mg/kg/day), and 100 mg/kg bid (200 mg/kg/day).
Daily doses were administered at least 6 hours, but not more than
7 hours, apart.

Route of administration:

Oral gavage

Formulation/Vehicle:

® in reverse osmosis deionized water with 3.0

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

Observations and Results: Main Study Groups
Parameters

Major findings

Mortality

200 mg/kg/day: There were two unscheduled deaths of male animals during
the dosing phase (Day 12 and Day 23) which were considered related to
ziftomenib administration. The cause of mortality/moribundity was
considered a secondary complication of ziftomenib-related clinical signs
(primarily, emesis- and/or excreta- related findings) rather than a primary
effect of the test article. Ziftomenib-related histopathological findings in
primarily the hepatobiliary organs, gastrointestinal tract, lymphoid organs,
and kidney also contributed to the demise of these two high-dose males.

Clinical signs

Ziftomenib-related clinical signs were mostly dose responsive and were

generally observed with the greatest frequency at the high-dose level. They
included included excreta-related findings (soft, liquid or mucoid feces and
feces with particles) and suspected dehydration (high-dose females). Limited
occurrences of vomitus were observed in both sexes at the mid- and highdose levels. Thin body condition was noted for three high-dose females
beginning on Day 18 and continuing through Day 29; single occurrences of
thin body condition were observed on the day of terminal necropsy for a
single male each at the mid- and high-dose levels.
Body weight

200 mg/kg/day: On Day 28, mean body weight values were 25.82% (males)
and 27.66% (females) lower than concurrent control values.
60 mg/kg/day: On Day 28, mean body weight values were 11.26% (males)

Food
consumption

200 mg/kg/day: In week 4 food consumption was statistically significantly
deceased in high dose male (-39.52%) and female (-39.87%) animals.
60 mg/kg/day: In mid dose male and female animals food consumption in
week 4 was decreased by 25.56% and 38.56%, respectively.

Ophthalmology No noteworthy findings
Electrocardiography

200 mg/kg/day: Potentially ziftomenib related decreases in mean heart rate
(within the normal range for the canine) were noted after dosing on Days 3
and 24 in high-dose females, prior to dosing on Day 24 in high-dose males,
and prior to dosing on Day 24 in low-dose males.

Hematology

200 mg/kg/day: Statistically significantly decreased reticulocyte count and
associated decreases in mean corpuscular volume and red cell distribution
with were observed in high dose animals of both sexes. The statistically
significantly increased white blood cell count noted for high-dose females
was due to increased neutrophil, monocyte, eosinophil, and large unstained
cell counts.

Gross
pathology

≥ 60 mg/kg/day: Macroscopic findings were noted in the mid and high dose
animals of both sexes in hepatobiliary organs (dilatation of bile duct, white
discoloration white foci, gritty content of/in the gall bladder, pale
discoloration and green discoloration of the liver), GI tract (dark red foci)
and lymphoid tissue (small thymus, spleen).

Organ weights

200 mg/kg/day: At terminal necropsy in males statistically significantly
decreased thymus and epididymis weights and increased liver weight were
noted at the high-dose level.
≥ 60 mg/kg/day: In females at the terminal necropsy changes in organ weight
included statistically significant decreases in splenic and thymus weights at
the mid- and high-dose levels, decreases in kidney and heart weights at the
high-dose level, and increased liver weight at the high-dose level.

Histopathology

At the terminal necropsy, ziftomenib-related microscopic findings involved
the hepatobiliary, gastrointestinal, renal, lymphoid, and hematopoietic
organs.

Observations and Results: Main Study Groups
The ziftomenib-related findings of basophilic accumulations and increased
cellularity of basophilic macrophages involving the liver, bile duct,
gallbladder, and intestines noted in mid- and high-dose males and females
were considered adverse. In addition,the increased cellularity of vacuolated
macrophages within the lymphoid organs and lymphoid depletion of the
thymus, Peyer’s patches, and lymph nodes in both sexes at the mid- and
high-dose levels and the membranous glomerulopathy were considered
adverse.

Other relevant

Toxicokinetics

Transmission electron microscopy (TEM) revealed an increased numbers
and/or larger lysosomes containing phospholipid- like material (in myelinoid
aggregates, lamellar, and/or multivesicular bodies) within the cytoplasm of
hepatocytes and Kupffer cells of ziftomenib-treated dogs when compared
with lysosomes of vehicle-treated control animals. These findings persisted
at the end of the recovery period, with somewhat decreased size and
incidence of lysosomes containing phospholipid material within hepatocytes
and Kupffer cells indicating partial recovery (ie, fewer lysosomes containing
nascent phospholipid material). Taken together, the increased incidence and
magnitude of lysosomal accumulation of phospholipid material in high-dose
animals are consistent with a diagnosis of phospholipidosis.
Systemic exposure to ziftomenib and KO-516 (metabolite) was independent
of sex. Mean Cmax and AUC0-24h values for ziftomenib increased with
increasing dose in an approximately dose-proportional manner on Day 1. On
Day 28, mean Cmax values for ziftomenib increased with increasing dose in
a less-than-dose-proportional manner while AUC0-24h values for ziftomenib
increased with increasing dose in an approximately dose-proportional
manner.
Mean Cmax and AUC0-24h values for KO-516 increased with increasing
dose in an approximately dose-proportional manner on Day 1 and in a lessthan-dose-proportional manner on Day 28. Systemic exposure to ziftomenib
and KO-516 generally appeared to increase following repeated
administration of ziftomenib at 20 and 60 mg/kg/day with no change at 200
mg/kg/day. Systemic exposure (AUC0-24h) to KO-516 was approximately
19% to 35% of the systemic exposure to ziftomenib.
Combined-sex systemic exposure (Cmax and AUC0-24h) to ziftomenib at the
HNSTD (60 mg/kg/day) on Day 28 was 1150 ng/mL and 22,500 ng*hr/mL,
respectively. Corresponding values for the KO-516 metabolite were 290
ng/mL and 5600 ng*hr/mL, respectively.

Observations and Results: Recovery Groups Day 56/57
Due to the unscheduled deaths of 2 males in Group 4, all surviving Group 4 males were
assigned to the terminal necropsy; all males in the vehicle control group were assigned to the
terminal necropsy due to no assessment of recovery in the males. Recovery data are only
available for the female anyimals.
Body weight
Food
consumption
Full recovery was observed Day 56/57 in high dose females.
Hematology
and
Coagulation
Findings in one high-dose (200 mg/kg/day) female included progression in
elevated ALP and GGT activities, persistently increased SDH activity,
persistently decreased serum albumin concentration, partial recovery in ALT,
and full recovery of elevated AST activity and serum total bilirubin
concentration. In the other high-dose female, full recovery of the increased
AST activity and total bilirubin concentration were noted, with partial
recovery of the increases in ALT, SDH, ALP, and GGT noted at the end of
Clinical
the dosing phase. However, in this female, there was further progression of
chemistry
the decreased serum albumin concentration.
The decreases in serum calcium concentration noted for both females at the
terminal collection had progressed in both animals by the end of the recovery
phase. Resolution of the decreased serum chloride and phosphorus
concentrations was noted female while the decreases persisted in the other
recovery female.
Gross
Small thymus was observed in the two female animals.

Systemic exposure to ziftomenib and its 2 metabolites, KO-516 and
KO-739, was generally similar between males and females. KO-516-toziftomenib ratios for overall daily exposure (in terms of AUCtlast) were
0.258 to 0.405 on both evaluation days. KO-739-to-ziftomenib ratios
for overall daily exposure (AUCtlast) were 0.913 to 1.57 on Day 1 and
1.79 to 6.25 on Day 91. Exposure (Cmax and AUCtlast) to all 3 analytes
generally increased with increasing dose from 6 to 60 mg/kg/day in a
greater-than-dose-proportional manner in both sexes on both Days 1
and 91. Exposure to KO-516 was nearly or approximately dose
proportional on Day 91, in terms of Cmax in males and both Cmax and
AUCtlast in females. There was no meaningful accumulation of
ziftomenib or KO-516 in plasma on Day 91 relative to Day 1. However,
accumulation of KO-739 in plasma was noted in both sexes on Day 91,
as indicated by mean accumulation ratios ranging from 2.04 to 5.78.
Systemic exposure (mean Cmax and AUCtlast) to ziftomenib at the
NOAEL/HNSTD (20 mg/kg/day) on Day 91 was 274 ng/mL and 5100
ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males and 271 ng/mL and 4930 ng*hr/mL,
respectively, in females. Corresponding values for the KO-516
metabolite were 104 ng/mL and 1740 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males
and 90.2 ng/mL and 1550 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in females.
Corresponding values for the KO-739 metabolite were 1510 ng/mL and
22,000 ng*hr/mL, respectively, in males and 1630 ng/mL and 24,300
ng*hr/mL, respectively, in females.

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
Food consumption
Hematology and

Clinical chemistry

Gross pathology
Histopathology

Effects were partially (body weight/body weight gain) or fully (food
consumption) recovered.
The decreases in hemoglobin, hematocrit, MCH, and MCHC persisted
in both sexes, with partial reversibility of RBC counts in both sexes and
MCV in females.
The increased ALP persisted in females, with partial reversibility of
ALP in males and ALT in both sexes. The decreased ALB had partially
reversed in both sexes while the increased GLOB persisted. Decreased
mean serum cholesterol (CHOL) showed partial reversibility.
Macroscopic findings included pale, green discoloration of the liver;
abnormal content, discoloration, and dilatation of the gallbladder; and
green discoloration and enlargement of the mesenteric lymph nodes.
Adverse microscopic findings in the hepatobiliary and lymphoid
systems persisted

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. Pivotal reports previously reviewed
are summarized below or described below if not previously reviewed.
In the previously reviewed 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study in mice (study number
00275510, reviewed under IND 142028), animals were treated twice daily by oral gavage with
ziftomenib at doses 40, 120, or 400 mg/kg/day. The high-dose was poorly tolerated, and animals
were given a 3-day dosing holiday due to signs of excessive toxicity. Dosing was resumed at 200
mg/kg/day. There were 3 unscheduled deaths in the high-dose male group that occurred on Days
17-22. All animals were euthanized in extremis due to adverse body weight loss and declining
clinical condition. Animals presented with clinical signs of severe dehydration, decreased
activity, and pale skin. The primary histological findings were macrophage infiltration and
vacuolation of the liver, lung, and lymphoid tissues. Additionally, there was an unscheduled
euthanasia of a female in the mid-dose group that was dehydrated with decreased activity, pale
skin, and was cold to the touch, but no clear cause morbidity was identified. Gross pathology
revealed discoloration in the liver (green) in animals treated with ≥ 120 mg/kg/day, which
correlated to other signs of liver toxicity, including increased transaminases, sorbitol
dehydrogenase, and bilirubin, increased liver weight, and macrophage infiltration. Microscopic
findings of cellular infiltration and vacuolation were observed in the lung and lymphoid tissues,
and minimal to moderate cytoplasmic rarefaction was observed in the kidney. A decrease in
erythrocyte counts was also observed with associated decreases in hematocrit and hemoglobin.
Observations made during FDA’s assessment of the 13-week toxicology study in mice, some of
which were not captured by the Applicant, are included in the Tables below. Seven animals from
the test article groups were found dead or euthanized in extremis. Of these, malignant lymphoma
was noted microscopically in the lymph nodes, thymus, bone marrow, heart, kidney, liver, lung,
89

KO-539 Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay / KO-TOX-0014 / Section 4.1 of Module 2.6.6
and Section 8 Module 2.6.7.
Key findings:
• Under the conditions of this study, ziftomenib was determined to be negative
(nonmutagenic) in the bacterial reverse mutation assay.
Study Type
In Vitro Reverse Mutation Assay in Bacterial Cells
GLP
Yes
Compliance
Test System
Plate incorporation method using S. typhimurium tester strains TA98,
TA100, TA1535, and TA1537 and E. coli tester strain WP2uv rA in the
presence and absence of metabolic activation (Aroclor™ 1254-induced rat
liver S9 microsomal fraction).
In the range-finding assay, ziftomenib was tested in single plates at 1.0, 5.0,
10, 50, 100, 500, 1000, and 5000 μg/plate with strains TA100 and WP2
uvrA. In the mutagenicity assay, ziftomenib was tested at 12.5, 25, 50, 100,
250, 500, 1000, and 2500 μg/plate.
Criteria for a negative response were met for all tester strains with and
without metabolic activation. The data from the vehicle and positive controls
demonstrated the validity and sensitivity of this test system for detecting
chemical mutagens with and without metabolic activation.
KO-539 In Vitro Micronucleus Assay in Cultured Human Peripheral Blood
Lymphocytes / KO-TOX-0015 / Section 4.2 of Module 2.6.6 and Section 8 Module 2.6.7.
Key findings:
• Under the conditions of this study, ziftomenib was considered negative
(nonclastogenic/aneugenic) when evaluated in the in vitro micronucleus assay conducted
in HPBLs.
Study Type
In Vitro Micronucleus Assay
GLP
Yes
Compliance
Test System
Ziftomenib was evaluated for the potential to induce micronuclei in HPBLs
during short (4-hour) and long (24-hour) incubations with or without an
exogenous metabolic activation system (Aroclor 1254-induced rat liver S9
microsomal fraction) in cultured human peripheral blood lymphocytes
(HPBL). The concentrations selected for micronucleus evaluation in all
treatments were based on cytotoxicity. Micronuclei were evaluated in 2000
binucleated cells per concentration.

An In Vivo Micronucleus Assay of KO-539 by Oral Gavage in CD-1 Mice / KO-TOX0016 / Section 4.3of Module 2.6.6 and Section 9 Module 2.6.7.

(ie, decreases in the ratio of polychromatic to total erythrocytes) was noted for either sex at

and

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
Study design:
Major Findings

Assessment of embryofetal development
Day of Mating: GD 0; animals were dose during GDs 6 through 15. Day of
Laparohystercetomy: GD 18
All dams survived to scheduled necropsy on GD 18. No remarkable
clinical observations were noted at any dose level during the dosing or
postdosing phases.
At 200 mg/kg/day: In dams mean body weight was significantly
decreased (-17.5% vs control) on GD 16. In addition, mean gravid
uterine weight was significantly decreased (-90.8% vs control). These
effects were considered secondary to the entirely resorbed litters noted
for 6 of 7 dams at 200 mg/kg/day.
Mean litter proportion of postimplantation loss was significantly
increased (+94.6% vs control) at 200 mg/kg/day, with a corresponding
lower mean number of live fetuses (0.4 per dam). Six of seven gravid
females in this group had entirely resorbed litters (primarily early
resorption), thus precluding evaluation of fetal weight at this dose
level. At 60 mg/kg/day, decreased (-8.9% to -11.1% vs control) mean
fetal body weight values (males, females, and combined sex) were
noted. Intrauterine survival at 20 and 60 mg/kg/day and intrauterine
growth at 20 mg/kg/day were unaffected by ziftomenib administration.
External malformations were limited to cleft palate (entire length) for 1
(1), 4 (2), and 1 (1) fetuses (litters) at 20, 60, and 200 mg/kg/day,
respectively.

An Oral (Gavage) Study of the Effects of KO-539 on Embryo-Fetal Development in Mice
/ KO-TOX-0018 / Section 6.2 of Module 2.6.6 and Section 13.1 Module 2.6.7.
Key Findings:
• Ziftomenib was well tolerated maternally. The maternal NOAEL was established at the
high dose level (60 mg/kg/day).
• Adverse effects on intrauterine growth and survival, as well as fetal external and skeletal
malformations, were noted at 60 mg/kg/day, resulting in a developmental NOAEL of 20
mg/kg/day.
GLP compliance: Yes
Methods
Dose and frequency
0 mg/kg bid (0 mg/kg/day), 3 mg/kg bid (6 mg/kg/day), 10 mg/kg bid
of dosing:
(20 mg/kg/day), and 30 mg/kg bid (60 mg/kg/day) during GDs 6
through 15. Daily doses were administered at least 6 hours, but not
more than 7 hours, apart.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant that fertility and early embryonic developmental studies
were not warranted for the proposed indication. The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment
of the embryo-fetal toxicity. The following assessment provides additional information to
support the statements regarding the data in the label.
In the embryo-fetal development study, pregnant mice received ziftomenib doses of 6, 20, or 60
mg/kg/day on gestation days 6-15, and the dosing period was followed by a period of no dosing
on gestation days 16-18. Maternal body weight and weight gains were lower in the high-dose
animals by the end of the dosing period and continued to the end of the recovery period. There
were no macroscopic findings compared to controls throughout the study. Post-implantation loss
was higher in the high-dose group and is due to the higher number of dead fetuses, which in turn
was mainly due to 1 high-dose group animal that had a litter entirely composed of dead fetuses

NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
The clinical pharmacology of ziftomenib was studied in patients with relapsed or refractory
(R/R) acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and healthy volunteers and assessed in modeling and
simulation analyses. The objectives of the clinical pharmacology program were to evaluate the
pharmacokinetics (PK), including absorption, metabolism, excretion, and the impact of extrinsic
factors such as drug-drug interactions (DDIs), and food, as well as intrinsic factors such as
hepatic and renal impairment.
Absorption: Ziftomenib is moderately permeable and is a substrate for BCRP and Pglycoprotein; however, PBPK modeling and clinical data have demonstrated that P-gp efflux or
inhibition effect on ziftomenib exposure would be minimal and not clinically meaningful.
Ziftomenib is rapidly absorbed (Tmax = 3.5 hours) after oral administration in the fasted state,
with an absolute bioavailability of 12.9%. A high-fat meal increased ziftomenib exposure by four
times and delayed absorption. Co-administration with rabeprazole, a gastric acid-reducing agent,
reduced ziftomenib exposure by up to 70%. PBPK modeling with esomeprazole demonstrated
that staggering the PPI dose by 2 hours after ziftomenib had no effect on ziftomenib AUC and by
≥12 hours before ziftomenib may reduce AUC by 14%, which is not considered clinically
meaningful. Thus, staggering PPI administration by 2 hours to 12 hours after the ziftomenib dose
may have minimal impact on ziftomenib exposure.
Distribution: Ziftomenib is highly protein-bound (>95%) in plasma, with a mean (%CV)
apparent volume of distribution of 30100 L (98.4%). Ziftomenib has mean (%CV) half-life of
61.5 hours (26.3%) and a moderate accumulation upon once daily multiple-dose administration.
Steady-state plasma levels are reached within 15 days.
Metabolism: Ziftomenib is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 (60% of hepatic metabolism) and
also by CYP1A2 and CYP2D6. Eight minor metabolites were identified, but ziftomenib remains
the major component in plasma, accounting for 25-29% of total drug-related radioactivity.
Elimination: The mean (%CV) apparent oral clearance is 339 L/hour (88.6%). The main route of
elimination is fecal (89.7%) with minimal renal excretion (0.525%).
Drug-Drug Interactions – Effect of Other Drugs on Ziftomenib PK:
Gastric Acid Reducing Agents: Administration of PPIs reduced ziftomenib AUC0-inf by 53% and
Cmax by 70% in healthy volunteers. Modeling demonstrated that staggering the PPI dose by 2
hours after ziftomenib administration had no impact on ziftomenib AUC while staggering it by
12 hours resulted in a 14% reduction in ziftomenib AUC.
CYP3A4 Inhibitors: Modeling demonstrated weak to moderate changes in ziftomenib exposure
with the CYP3A4 inhibitors. A strong CYP3A4 inhibitor (itraconazole) increases ziftomenib
121
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
AUC 2.9 times and Cmax 2.1 times, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor (fluconazole) increases
ziftomenib AUC 1.8 times and Cmax 1.6 times, and a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor (cimetidine)
increases ziftomenib AUC by 36% and Cmax by 37%. However, as safety exposure-response (ER) analysis demonstrated that there were no statistically significant (ie, all p‑values >0.05) E-R
relationships with any ziftomenib model-predicted steady-state exposure measures and adverse
events (AEs), the increase in ziftomenib exposure with CYP inhibitors is not considered to be
clinically relevant. Ziftomenib can be co-administered with strong, moderate, or weak CYP3A4
inhibitors.
CYP3A4 Inducers: Modeling demonstrated that interactions with strong or moderate CYP3A4
inducers are likely to be moderate (AUCtau,ss decrease by ≥50% – <80%). Interactions with weak
CYP3A4 inducers are likely to be weak (AUCtau,ss ≥20% – <50%). Co-administration of
ziftomenib with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inducers should be avoided.
CYP1A2 Inhibitors and CYP2D6 Inhibitors: Modeling demonstrated a weak interaction between
ziftomenib and either CYP1A2 or CYP2D6 inhibitors. Ziftomenib can be co-administered with
CYP1A2 or CYP2D6 inhibitors.
Transporter Inhibitors: Ziftomenib is a substrate for P-gp and BCRP but not a substrate of BSEP
or MRP2. Modeling demonstrated minimal to no impact of a P-gp inhibitor on the ziftomenib CL
and volume. No clinically meaningful effect on ziftomenib is expected. Ziftomenib can be coadministered with P-gp inhibitors.
Drug-Drug Interactions – Effect of Ziftomenib on the PK of Other Drugs:
Based on modeling, no DDI was demonstrated with the CYP3A4 substrates midazolam or
venetoclax or the UGT1A1 substrate raltegravir. Ziftomenib can be co-administered with
sensitive CYP3A4 and UGT1A1 substrates.
Ziftomenib does not inhibit P-gp, BCRP, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, MATE2-K,
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, BSEP, or MRP2.
Efficacy Exposure-Response (E-R) Analysis: No statistically significant relationships were
found between steady-state ziftomenib exposure (AUC, Cmax, Ctrough) and efficacy endpoints
(e.g., overall response rate or complete remission) in patients with R/R NPM1-m AML.
Safety Exposure-Response (E-R) Analysis: No statistically significant correlation was found
between steady-state ziftomenib exposure (AUC, Cmax, Ctrough) and treatment-emergent adverse
events (TEAEs) or severe adverse events (Grade 4+) in patients with R/R NPM1-m AML.
Similarly, no meaningful relationship was observed for other safety endpoints. No covariates had
a significant or clinically relevant effect on treatment-related adverse events (TRAEs).
Potential Effect on Cardiac Repolarization: The potential for QTc prolongation with ziftomenib
was evaluated in an open-label Phase 1 study at doses ranging from 100 mg to 1000 mg once
daily (0.17 to 1.7 times the approved recommended dosage) in 31 patients with relapsed or
refractory AML. At the recommended KOMZIFTI dose of 600 mg once daily, no large mean

changes in the QTc interval (>20 ms) were observed; the mean increase in QTcF was 8.25 ms in
patients with relapsed or refractory AML.
The concentration-QTc analysis of ziftomenib showed that at a steady-state plasma concentration
of 865 ng/mL, the upper bound of the 90% confidence interval for ΔQTcF is below 10 ms,
indicating no clinically relevant QTc prolongation at these levels. At a higher concentration of
1380 ng/mL, the upper bound exceeds 20 ms, suggesting a larger change in QTc; however, this
concentration is much higher than what is typically observed in clinical settings. Overall, the
QTc analysis confirms that ziftomenib does not cause clinically significant QTc prolongation
within its therapeutic concentration range. No significant effects were observed on heart rate, PR
interval, or QRS interval, further supporting the safety of ziftomenib regarding cardiac
repolarization and conduction.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology of ziftomenib has been studied in patients with R/R AML and in
healthy volunteers, as well as assessed using modeling and simulation analysis. The clinical data
along with NCA, PPK, ER, PBPK and concentration-QT analyses allowed for adequate
characterization of ziftomenib ADME and PK, and assess intrinsic and extrinsic factors affecting
ziftomenib exposure and impact on safety and efficacy of ziftomenib.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of ziftomenib and metabolite (KO739 and KO-516) PK. For a summary of FDA’s assessment on the effect of intrinsic and
extrinsic factors on ziftomenib PK, see Section 6.2.2.2.
For detailed information, see Section 6.3.1.
Exposure-Response (E-R): The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of E-R
analyses for safety and efficacy to support the proposed dosage for the target indication. For
detailed information, see Section 14.4.3.
QTc prolongation: The FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s assessment that ziftomenib has no
clinically relevant effect on QTc. In R/R AML patients receiving ziftomenib 600 mg QD, a
concentration-dependent increase in QTc was observed. For more detailed information, see
Section 6.3.1.
Outstanding issues: There are several outstanding issues for which PMR/PMCs will be issued, as
detailed in Section 6.2.2.3 and FDA Table 22.

General Dosing
