NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
In Part 1a of Study KO-MEN-001, 30 patients with relapsed/refractory (R/R) AML were treated
with ziftomenib in doses ranging from 50 to 1000 mg daily, establishing a maximum tolerated
dose (MTD) of 800 mg/day. Based on safety, tolerability, PK and preliminary clinical activity
observed in Part 1a, the 200 and 600 mg QD doses were selected for randomized comparison in
Part 1b to determine the RP2D.
In Part 1b of Study KO-MEN-001, efficacy data from the randomized 200 mg and 600 mg doses
of ziftomenib in patients with NPM1-m and KMT2A-r AML showed that the 600 mg QD dose
had superior efficacy. No responses were reported at the 200 mg ziftomenib dose level, which,
per protocol, led to the closure of this dose level to further enrollment due to futility. In contrast,
the 600 mg ziftomenib dose level demonstrated clinical activity in both NPM1-m and KMT2A-r
R/R AML. Here, the overall CR/CRh, composite complete remission (CRc), and ORR rates were
25.0%, 30.6%, and 33.3% respectively, with the majority of patients who were evaluated locally
for MRD in each of the response categories also reaching MRD negativity. For Part 1b patients
with NPM1-m AML treated with 600 mg ziftomenib, the durable CR and CRc rates were 35%
and 40%, respectively. Safety data comparing the 200 mg and 600 mg doses of ziftomenib in
patients with NPM1-m and KMT2A-r AML demonstrated no clear relationship between
ziftomenib dose and safety profile.
PK data showed a three-fold increase in plasma exposure at steady state with the 600 mg dose
compared to the 200 mg dose. E-R analysis indicated a trend toward higher response rates with
increasing exposure in the NPM1-m patient population, though the results were not statistically
significant. In patients with NPM1-m R/R AML, this increased exposure did not correlate with
any additional safety concerns, including severe adverse events (AEs) or dose-limiting toxicity
(DLT). Thus, the totality of clinical data and modeling supported 600 mg QD as the RP2D in
R/R NPM1-m AML.
In the Phase 2 registrational portion of Study KO-MEN-001 in patients with R/R NPM1-m AML
at 600 mg QD ziftomenib achieved the primary endpoint, with a CR/CRh rate of 21.7% (95% CI:
13.8 - 31.6), which demonstrated a statistically significant improvement (p=0.0058) in CR/CRh
rate relative to the 12% historical control. The clinical benefit of 600 mg QD ziftomenib was
further exemplified by its robust activity across all evaluated subgroups and key secondary
endpoints. At 600 mg QD, the TEAEs were consistent with what is expected in an R/R AML
population. The most common TEAEs included febrile neutropenia, differentiation syndrome
(DS), and sepsis. Among these, 1.1% of febrile neutropenia cases were ziftomenib-related, all
DS cases were linked to ziftomenib, and no sepsis cases were ziftomenib related. No deaths were
attributed to ziftomenib-related TEAEs. E-R analysis demonstrated no statistically significant
correlations between ziftomenib steady-state PK parameters (AUC, Cmax or Ctrough) and
probability of response (efficacy or safety).
The Applicant’s Position:
The recommended dosage of ziftomenib of 600 mg taken orally once daily was selected based on
the totality of data demonstrating a favorable benefit-risk balance in Phase 1, where a range of

doses were tested, and Phase 2. This dosing regimen is supported by the pharmacokinetic

The integration of ziftomenib exposure-response findings for both efficacy and safety support the
recommended dose of 600 mg QD dose of ziftomenib as the optimal regimen, maximizing

The FDA’s Assessment:

favorable risk-benefit profile for ziftomenib 600 mg QD in patients with NPM1-m R/R AML.
The 600 mg dosage demonstrated improved activity versus the 200 mg dosage (CR + CRh 25%
vs 0%). The dose-response (D-R) and E-R safety relationships for ziftomenib are relatively flat,
supporting the proposed 600 mg dose.
For more detailed information, see Sections 6.3.2.1 and 6.3.2.2.
6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

Data:
Impact of intrinsic factors on ziftomenib pharmacokinetics (PK) was assessed in the population
PK model (PPK) analysis. Disease state (AML patient vs. healthy volunteers) was identified as a
statistically significant covariate. However, since dose optimization was specifically conducted
in patients with NPM1-m AML, no further dose adjustments were required. The PPK analysis
indicated that intrinsic factors such as age (ranging 18 to 86 years), body weight, height, BMI,
BSA, eGFR, serum creatinine, bilirubin, albumin, ALP, ALT, AST, sex, race, ethnicity, mutation
type, mild or moderate renal impairment, and mild or moderate hepatic impairment did not
significantly affect the PK of ziftomenib. Therefore, no therapeutic individualization or dose
adjustment is needed based on these factors.
Ziftomenib has not been studied in pediatric patients.
The PPK analysis demonstrated that mild (n=32, 17%) and moderate (n=5, 2.7%) hepatic
impairment, as classified by the NCI Organ Dysfunction Working Group, did not significantly
affect ziftomenib exposure. The effect of severe hepatic impairment on ziftomenib’s PK has not
been assessed (no patient enrolled in study KO-MEN-001).
Given the low renal excretion of ziftomenib (only 0.525% of the radioactive dose was excreted
in urine) demonstrated in the completed mass balance study (KO-MEN-005), renal impairment is
not expected to significantly influence ziftomenib’s PK, which is confirmed by the PPK analysis
showed that mild (n=72, 38.3%) and moderate (n=21, 11.2%) renal impairment did not notably
affect the PK parameters of ziftomenib. The impact of severe renal impairment (eGFR ≤29
mL/min) on ziftomenib’s PK was not investigated (no patient enrolled in study KO-MEN-001).

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
A high-fat meal increased ziftomenib exposure by four times and delayed absorption; therefore,
ziftomenib is recommended to be administered in the fasted state.
Co-administration with rabeprazole, a gastric acid-reducing agent, reduced ziftomenib exposure
by up to 70%. PBPK modeling with esomeprazole demonstrated that staggering the PPI dose by
2 hours after ziftomenib had no effect on ziftomenib AUC and by ≥12 hours before ziftomenib
may reduce AUC by 14%, which is not considered clinically meaningful. Thus, staggering PPI
administration by 2 hours to 12 hours after the ziftomenib dose would have minimal impact on
ziftomenib exposure. No adjustments for ziftomenib are needed with co-administration of gastric
acid reducing agents.
Additional extrinsic factors were the subject of PPK and PBPK analysis which indicated that
interactions with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inducers are expected to cause a reduction in
ziftomenib exposure (AUCtau,ss decreasing by ≥50% to <80%). Interactions with weak CYP3A4
inducers are likely to result in a smaller effect, with AUCtau,ss decreasing by ≥20% to <50%.
Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors have been identified as a significant covariate on ziftomenib CL,
leading to an approximately two-fold increase in ziftomenib exposure, with Cmin, Cmax, and AUC
values being 2.44-, 1.96-, and 2.18-fold higher, respectively. Despite this increase in exposure,
safety and exposure-response (E-R) analysis showed no statistically significant relationships (all
p>0.05) between ziftomenib model-predicted steady-state exposure measures (AUCss, Cmax,ss,
and Cmin,ss) and adverse events (AEs). As a result, the increase in ziftomenib exposure with
CYP3A4 inhibitors is not considered clinically relevant, and no dose adjustment is required
when ziftomenib is co-administered with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors. No DDI was
predicted with the CYP2D6 inhibitor bupropion. Interactions with strong CYP1A2 inhibitors
(fluvoxamine) are likely to be weak.
Coadministration of strong and moderate CYP3A inducers is to be avoided.
The Applicant’s Position:
The recommended dosage of ziftomenib is 600 mg taken orally once daily in the fasted state. No
dose adjustment is required based on age, body weight, sex, race, mild or moderate renal or
hepatic impairment, co-administration with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors or with
CYP1A2 or CYP2D6 inhibitors. The impact of severe renal or hepatic impairment on ziftomenib
PK has not been studied. Co-administration of ziftomenib with strong or moderate CYP3A
(b) (4)
inducers should be avoided, and

Expected High
Clinical Exposure
Scenario

There is an approximately 4-fold increase in ziftomenib Cmax and AUC
when taken with high fat meal. Hence the expected high Cmax-ss is
approximately 3524 ng/mL at 600 mg QD. However, ziftomenib is
recommended to be taken in fasted state.

Nonclinical Safety
Pharmacology
Studies (S7B)

In a GLP compliant in vitro hERG channel assay (KO-TOX-0023),
ziftomenib IC50 for inhibitory effect on hERG was 0.066 µM. At the
RP2D of 600 mg QD, the steady state Cmax was 881 ng/mL (1.22 µM).
The expected high clinical exposure at this dose with high fat meal is
expected to be approximately 4.88 µM. Given that ziftomenib has >99%
protein binding, the free drug Cmax-ss will be approximately 0.049 µM,
which is 1.3-fold lower than the IC50.
In a GLP complaint CV assessment in conscious radiotelemetrized
beagle dogs (KO-TOX-0022) at 40, 120 and 200 mg/kg ziftomenib dose
demonstrated that there were no ziftomenib-related effects on the QRS
complex, RR, QT, or QTc interval durations at any ziftomenib dose level
evaluated. No changes in ECG waveform morphology were considered a
result of ziftomenib administration at any dose level.

Clinical Cardiac
Safety

Monotherapy (KO-MEN-001):
Based on concentration-QT interval corrected for heart rate (QTcF)
analysis (Study KO-PK-0003) of ECGs from 31 patients in Study KOMEN-001 Parts 1a and 1b, a ∆QTcF increase with 90% upper
confidence interval (UCI) exceeding 10 ms and 20 ms can be predicted
for ziftomenib plasma concentration of approximately 865 ng/mL and
1380 ng/mL, respectively. At steady state, ziftomenib mean Cmax was
881 ng/mL at 600 mg once daily (QD), demonstrating that ziftomenib is
not expected to have a clinically significant effect on QTc prolongation.
Categorical analysis demonstrated that there were no QTcF prolongation
>480 ms, >500 ms, or ΔQTcF >60 ms. Ziftomenib also had no clinically
significant effect on heart rate or on cardiac conduction (ie, the PR or
QRS interval). In summary, the results from the cardiodynamic

The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology of ziftomenib was assessed in in vitro studies, in clinical studies in
patients with R/R AML and healthy volunteers, as well as in modeling and simulation analyses.
The objectives of the clinical pharmacology program were to evaluate the pharmacokinetics
(PK), including absorption, metabolism, excretion, and the impact of extrinsic factors such as
drug-drug interactions (DDIs), and food, as well as intrinsic factors such as hepatic and renal
impairment. Exposure-response and -safety relationships were modeled using data from Study
KO-MEN-001, as was a concentration-QT evaluation.
Based on the extent and results of these studies and assessments, the clinical pharmacology of
ziftomenib was well characterized to inform the overall pharmacokinetic profile and provide
guidance for using ziftomenib in the proposed USPI.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that ziftomenib PK are adequate to
inform the overall PK profile and provide guidance for ziftomenib use.
General PK
Exposure: The FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s position regarding mean exposure, as it
does not account for coadministration of CYP3A inhibitors, which were commonly used in KOMEN-001, on ziftomenib PK.
The FDA assessed steady state PK of ziftomenib (C2D1) at 600 mg (the approved dosage) when
administered with strong CYP3A inhibitors or without CYP3A inhibitors. The FDA analysis
utilized population PK (PopPK)-derived Cycle 2 Day 1 Cmax and AUC values. Coadministration
of a strong CYP3A inhibitor was defined as any administration of the strong CYP3A inhibitor
within 3 days prior to the C2D1 ziftomenib PK sample. Patients with concomitant PPI use on the
day of ziftomenib administration were excluded from the analysis given the known DDI
observed with PPI administration.
Steady-state maximum plasma concentration (Cmax; Cmax,ss) was 617 (33%) ng/mL when coadministered with strong CYP3A inhibitors, compared to 288 (41%) ng/mL when administered
without strong CYP3A inhibitors. Steady-state area under the concentration-time curve at last
sampling time (AUC; AUClast,ss) was 13,185 (36%) ng*h/mL when co-administered with strong
CYP3A inhibitors, compared to 5,512 (48%) ng*h/mL when administered without strong
CYP3A inhibitors.
Dose proportionality: The FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s assessment of doseproportionality over 50 to 600 mg due to the limited sample size at lower doses (i.e., n=1 for

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
each 50 mg and 100 mg) and confounded by DDIs (i.e., coadministration with CYP3A inhibitors
and ARAs) in the Phase 1a dose escalation cohorts.
The FDA assessed dose proportionality of single-dose (Cycle 1 Day 1) PK in the Phase 1a/b dose
escalation and expansion cohort with patients coadministered strong CYP3A inhibitors but not
ARAs. Cohorts with n ≥ 3 per dose were evaluated. Dose-proportionality could not be assessed
in patients not coadministered strong CYP3A inhibitors due to an insufficient number of patients
at each dose level.
The power model slope estimates (95% CI) were 0.92 (-0.06, 1.90) for AUC at 24 hours
(AUC24), 1.06 (0.14, 1.99) for AUC over the dosing interval (AUCt), and 0.74 (0.18, 1.67) for
Cmax. AUC was dose-proportional and Cmax was slightly less than dose-proportional over a dose
range of 200 mg (0.3 times the approved dose) to 600 mg (the approved dose).
Accumulation: The FDA recommends reporting accumulation based on a comparison of singledose versus steady-state exposures observed in the 600 mg QD phase 1b expansion cohort,
which indicates an approximately 3-fold accumulation after 28 days of dosing (Cmax
accumulation ratio [AR]=2.7, AUC24 AR=3.4)
Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, Elimination, Transport
Absorption: The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that ziftomenib absolute BA is low
(13%), despite moderate in vitro permeability (apparent permeability [Papp]=2.69 x 10-6 cm/s).
The FDA recommends reporting time to Cmax (Tmax) based on that observed at Cycle 2 Day 1 in
the 600 mg QD phase 1b expansion cohort, for which Tmax was 4.1 hours (0.5 to 8.2 hours).
Distribution: The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that ziftomenib is > 99% bound
to plasma proteins (mainly albumin) with blood-to-plasma partition coefficient (KB/P)=0.60 to
0.73 and apparent volume of distribution (Vz/F)=30,100 L (98%).
Metabolism: The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that ziftomenib is primarily
metabolized by CYP3A, and the parent compound accounts for the majority of circulating drugrelated exposure.
Elimination: The FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s assessment of the elimination PK
values, because it does not account for coadministration of CYP3A inhibitors, which were
commonly used in KO-MEN-001.
The FDA conducted the following assessment to characterize steady state (Cycle 2 Day 1) t1/2
and apparent oral clearance (CL/F) of ziftomenib when administered with strong CYP3A
inhibitors or without CYP3A inhibitors. The FDA analysis utilized PopPK-derived Cycle 2 Day
1 PK values. Coadministration of a strong CYP3A inhibitor was defined as any administration of
the strong CYP3A inhibitor within 3 days prior to the Cycle 2 Day 1 ziftomenib PK sample.
Patients with concomitant PPI use on the day of ziftomenib administration were excluded from
the analysis given the known DDI observed with PPI administration.

In Phase 1 Part 1b (NPM1-m and KMT2A-r), no CR + CRh responses were observed at the 200
mg dosage (n=17), leading to the 200 mg cohort being closed for futility. In comparison, the CR
+ CRh rate was 25% at the 600 mg dosage (n=36).
In Phase 2 (NPM1-m), the CR + CRh rate at 600 mg was 19% (n=92), which is similar to that
observed in Phase 1 Part 1b.
E-R Analysis: The Applicant conducted E-R analyses for efficacy in n=100 NPM1-m R/R AML
patients. The Applicant assessed several combinations of response endpoints, including
complete/partial response (CR, CRmrd-, or CRh) and complete/partial response (CR, CRmrd-).
No significant relationship was observed between ziftomenib exposure and response. However,
the flat E-R analyses were likely impacted by the limited patient sample size at dose levels other
than 600 mg (n=1 at 50, 100, and 100 mg; n < 12 at 400 and 800 mg; n= 23 at 200 mg) and low
CR/CRh overall [Section 14.4.3].
6.3.2.2 Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population
for which the indication is being sought?
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes, the proposed dosing regimen of 600 mg QD demonstrated efficacy in the indicated
population with a manageable safety profile in Study KO-MEN-001. The ziftomenib 600 mg QD
dose demonstrated superior efficacy compared to the 200 mg QD dose in the comparative Phase
1 evaluation leading to the recommended phase 2 dose selection. In the Phase 2 registrational
study the robust efficacy and manageable safety profile of 600 mg QD dose was confirmed in
R/R NPM1-m AML patients, as described in Section 6.2.2.1. Clinical evaluation indicated a food
effect and interaction with proton-pump inhibitors, however, additional modeling informed that
staggered administration of PPIs would not have a clinically meaningful effect. The PPK
analyses did not reveal any clinically meaningful effect of intrinsic factors such as age (ranging
18 to 86 years), body weight, height, BMI, BSA, eGFR, serum creatinine, bilirubin, albumin,
ALP, ALT, AST, sex, race, ethnicity, mutation type, mild or moderate renal impairment, and
mild or moderate hepatic impairment. Importantly, modeling also indicated there was no
clinically meaningful effect on ziftomenib exposure with co-administered CYP inhibitors. Thus,
ziftomenib dose adjustment is not required for these intrinsic and extrinsic factors. Ziftomenib at
the recommended dose of 600 mg QD demonstrates a favorable benefit-risk profile for patients
with R/R NPM1-m AML.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the recommended dose and regimen of 600 mg daily based on assessments
of efficacy, safety, and PK.
Of note, dose-finding was performed irrespective of coadministration of CYP3A-inhibiting azole
antifungals. Coadministration of strong CYP3A-inhibiting azoles was widespread for KO-MEN001 participants. Therefore, D-R and E-R relationships are driven primarily by ziftomenib

Efficacy
See Section 6.3.2.1.
Safety
The D-R and E-R safety relationships for ziftomenib were flat, supporting the proposed 600 mg
dose. Of note, the proposed dose is 1 dose level below the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) of
800 mg.
D-R Analysis: In Phase 1 Part 1a, there were no clear D-R relationships for treatment-emergent
adverse event (TEAE) (97%), grade ≥ 3 TEAE (83%), grade 5 TEAE (20%), serious adverse
event (SAE, 77%), dose interruption (23%), or treatment discontinuation (23%). The doselimiting toxicities (DLTs) included differentiation syndrome (n=1 at 1000 mg) and pneumonitis
(n=1 at 400 mg). 800 mg was determined to be the MTD following a DLT in 1 patient at 1000
mg.
In Phase 1 Part 1b, there were no clear D-R relationship between 200 mg and 600 mg for TEAE
(100% at both doses), grade ≥ 3 TEAE (94% at both doses), grade 5 TEAE (41 vs 28%), SAE
(82% vs 83%), dose interruption (41 vs 44%), treatment discontinuation (12 vs 28%), febrile
neutropenia (41% vs 17%), or differentiation syndrome (35% vs 31%). There were no clear D-R
relationships for DLTs, including differentiation syndrome (17% vs 14%) and cardiomyopathy
(0% vs 3.4%).
E-R Analysis: The Applicant conducted E-R analyses for safety in 174 patients. There were no
significant relationships between ziftomenib exposure and grade ≥ 3 TEAE; grade ≥ 4 TEAE;
grade ≥ 3 TRAE; grade ≥ 4 TRAE; grade ≥ 3 anemia, febrile neutropenia, neutropenia,
pneumonia, sepsis, thrombocytopenia, differentiation syndrome; or treatment-related
differentiation syndrome [Section 14.4.3].
6.3.2.3 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for
subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors (e.g. race, ethnicity, age,
performance status, genetic subpopulations, etc.)?
The Applicant’s Position:
No, there is no dose adjustment needed based on modeling assessment of intrinsic factors.
The effects of age, body weight, height, BMI, BSA, sex, race, ethnicity, AML mutation, eGFR,
serum creatinine, bilirubin, albumin, ALP, ALT, AST, renal impairment mild or moderate
((CLCr) 50-80 and (CLCr) 30-50 mL/min) or hepatic impairment mild or moderate (Child-Pugh
Class A and B) were evaluated by population PK modeling. None of these covariates had an
impact on ziftomenib PK, as discussed in Section 6.2.1. The effect of severe hepatic impairment

The Applicant has an ongoing KO-MEN-001 Sub-study evaluating the effect of ziftomenib on
PK of midazolam (CYP3A substrate), for which a PMR will be issued to complete (FDA Table
22). However, coadministration of moderate CYP3A inhibitors (i.e., azoles) are permitted in the
study, which are expected to have significant impact on midazolam PK, so data analysis may be
complicated by confounding DDIs.
CYP1A2 Inhibitors: The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that no dose adjustment is
required when used concomitantly with CYP1A2 inhibitors.
Although ziftomenib was also metabolized by CYP1A2 in vitro, furafylline (CYP1A2 inhibitor)
did not significantly reduce ziftomenib metabolism in human liver microsomes (HLMs),
suggesting that CYP1A2 does not play a major role in ziftomenib disposition.
CYP2D6 Inhibitors: The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that no dose adjustment is
required when used concomitantly with CYP2D6 inhibitors.
Although ziftomenib was also metabolized by CYP2D6 in vitro, quinidine (CYP2D6 inhibitor)
did not significantly reduce ziftomenib metabolism in HLMs, suggesting that CYP2D6 does not
play a major role in ziftomenib disposition.
Transporter DDIs
P-gp Inhibitors: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that no dosage
adjustment is needed when used concomitantly with P-gp inhibitors.
The Applicant’s PopPK analysis indicated that concomitant use with P-gp inhibitors on the same
day of ziftomenib administration has no clinically significant effect on ziftomenib PK [Section
19.4.2.2]. However, this analysis may be limited, as dosing time of P-gp inhibitor to ziftomenib
is not recorded and there is the potential for overlapping substrate specificity, as many P-gp
inhibitors are also CYP3A inhibitors. Additionally, the majority of patients received concurrent
posaconazole, which is a strong CYP3A and P-gp inhibitor, and no dose adjustment is
recommended with concurrent posaconazole based on clinical efficacy and safety data.
BCRP Inhibitors: The Applicant did not submit an assessment to evaluate the impact of BCRP
inhibitors on ziftomenib PK. A PMR will be issued to this conduct this assessment (FDA Table
22).
ARAs
The FDA conducted an analysis to evaluate the potential patient-centric impact of ARA usage.
Initially, there was not restriction on ARA use in KO-MEN-001. Following Protocol Version 6
(05/12/2022), use of PPI was prohibited, and staggered dosing of H2RAs (10 hours before or 2
hours after KOMZIFTI administration) was recommended. The FDA evaluated the distribution
of PPI and H2RA use in the KO-MEN-001 mITT population, as shown in FDA Figure 4. The

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
The Applicant’s Position:
The primary evidence of efficacy and safety for ziftomenib in adult patients with R/R AML with
NPM1 mutations is based on the ongoing Phase 1/2 study, KO-MEN-001. The primary analysis
of the Phase 2 cohort (N=92) with a data cutoff date of 28 OCT 2024 serves as the primary basis
of efficacy for the proposed indication. The study is supported by confirmatory evidence from
the Phase 1b portion of KO-MEN-001 which enrolled 20 R/R NPM1-m AML patients treated at
the proposed dose of 600 mg QD. The primary safety population supporting the proposed
indication is based on the total of 112 patients across these 2 studies.
Supportive safety information is derived from other ongoing clinical studies of ziftomenib. The
KO-MEN-001 sub-studies are evaluating drug-drug interactions in non-NPM1-m AML (substudies 1 and 2) or ziftomenib activity in acute lymphoblastic leukemia (sub-study 3), and
Studies KO-MEN-007 and KO-MEN-008 evaluate ziftomenib in combination with standard-ofcare regimens (eg, venetoclax, azacitidine, intensive and low-dose chemotherapy, and
gilteritinib). As these data do not directly inform the proposed indication of patients with R/R
NPM1-m AML due to the inherent heterogeneity of combination agents, molecular types and
study populations, summary-level data are presented in the application for a comprehensive
package.
The clinical studies supporting the efficacy and safety of ziftomenib are summarized above and
have been included in the submission.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of their Clinical Studies relevant to this
Application. The FDA review was primarily based on data from NDA 220305, relevant
published literature, and relevant information in the public domain.
Study KO-MEN-001 is the pivotal study that was the basis for establishing the safety and
efficacy of ziftomenib in patients with R/R NPM1-m AML. The Applicant submitted a complete
dataset for this study, using a data cut date of 10/28/2024, at the time of the initial NDA
submission (03/31/2025). This data set forms the basis for most of this clinical review. Updated
efficacy and safety using a data cut of 03/21/2025 were also utilized as part of the safety update.
Review Strategy
The FDA’s Assessment:
At the time of the NDA submission, the Applicant submitted datasets from 5 additional studies in
patients with AML and 4 studies in healthy volunteers. Data from the additional studies were
used to supplement the analysis of safety.

Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
KO-MEN-001

Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Basic Study Design
KO-MEN-001 is an ongoing Phase 1/2, first-in-human, open-label, multiple cohort study of the
menin inhibitor, ziftomenib, in adult patients with R/R AML. A graphical representation of the
study is provided in Applicant Figure 1.
The Phase 1 portion consisted of 2 parts: dose-escalation (Part 1a) and dose-validation/expansion
(Part 1b). In the Part 1a portion of the study, 30 R/R AML patients received ziftomenib in doses
ranging from 50 to 1000 mg QD and an MTD of 800 mg daily was established. Based on safety,
tolerability, PK, and preliminary antileukemic activity observed in Part 1a, Part 1b was initiated
for expansion at 2 dose levels (200 mg and 600 mg QD) in a total of 53 patients with KMT2A-r
or NPM1-m AML, to determine the RP2D. Based on the totality of efficacy, safety, and PK data
after completing Phase 1, the RP2D of ziftomenib was determined to be 600 mg QD for adult
patients with R/R NPM1-m AML administered in continuous 28-day cycles.
The Phase 2 portion of KO-MEN-001 was a single-cohort study evaluating ziftomenib 600 mg
QD in patients with locally determined R/R NPM1-m AML. The Phase 2 study is the primary
basis of efficacy for this application; the primary analysis was performed when the planned
sample size of 85 patients had been followed for at least 6 cycles or discontinued earlier, as
prespecified in the study protocol and statistical analysis plan. Patients could continue treatment
with ziftomenib until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity, and patients were permitted to
resume treatment with ziftomenib following hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT).

Trial Location and Applicability of Foreign Data
The ziftomenib clinical development program was designed globally and encompassed studies
conducted worldwide. The pivotal Phase 2 portion of Study KO-MEN-001 was conducted at 40
study centers in 7 countries including the US, Canada, Belgium, France, Germany, Italy, and
Spain. The standards of care and treatment of patients with R/R NPM1-m AML are similar for
patients located across all regions included in the clinical studies applicable to this NDA.
Furthermore, ziftomenib PK analyses assessed race and ethnicity as intrinsic factors with no
clinically relevant findings based on these variables. In accordance with 21 CFR 312.120, Good
Clinical Practice, compliance statements are provided for sites participating in the studies
included in the ziftomenib development program to support the quality and integrity of the
clinical study data from sites outside of the US as applicable to the assessment of the safety and

Administrative Structure
A data monitoring committee (DMC) monitored emerging data throughout Phase 1 and an
independent DMC was established for Phase 2 to periodically review emerging safety data to
identify if any safety or other concerns arise that may have affected patient or study continuation,
per the charter.
Study Treatment
Ziftomenib capsules were orally administered in continuous 28-day cycles at a dosage of 600 mg
once daily (QD). Ziftomenib was administered in the fasted state (1 hour before or 2 hours after a
meal). No dose adjustments were required for concomitant administration of CYP3A4 inhibitors.
Study Assessments
The Phase 2 Schedule of Assessments is provided in the KO-MEN-001 Protocol, Table 4. Key
efficacy and safety assessments included:
•
•
•
•

Blood, bone marrow aspirate or biopsy collection for response assessment on the last day
of each cycle (±5 days). Once clearance was documented, BM sampling could occur
every 3 months thereafter.
Blood sampling for hematology, chemistry, and coagulation safety assessment at
screening; Cycle 1 Day 1, Day 4, Day 8, Day 10, Day 15; and Day 1 of every cycle
thereafter.
Triplicate 12-lead ECGs at screening; Cycle 1 Day 1, Day 8, Day 15; and Day 1 of every
cycle thereafter.
Directed physical examination and vital signs at each study visit.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the description of KO-MEN-001. Although the study design
allowed patients to resume treatment with ziftomenib following HSCT, the study protocol only
required minimum conditions for post-HSCT eligibility, such as minimal count recovery (ANC ≥
0.5×109/L and platelets ≥ 20×109/L), non-hematologic transplant AEs < grade 2, no active
GVHD, a maximum of 0.5 mg/kg of prednisone equivalent, and stable doses of calcineurin
inhibitors. The study was neither designed nor conducted to evaluate the benefit and risk profile
of such post HSCT resumption.
The bone marrow aspirate or biopsy samples were collected monthly before the patients
achieved at least MLFS, every three months thereafter, and as needed if there were signs of
relapse. CBCs were conducted at least weekly until patients achieved clearance of leukemia in
the bone marrow and then at least monthly thereafter. Since bone marrow sampling would switch
157
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
to every three months based on clearance of blasts irrespective of peripheral count recovery, the
window between bone marrow showing blast clearance (< 5% blasts) and the CBC showing
blood count recovery might be as long as one month or even longer. This was reflected in the
investigators’ reported responses in the submitted datasets. The FDA adjudicated all the
responses with a two-step approach based on the schedule of assessments and clinical judgement.
First, FDA applied a window of -7 days to +14 days for peripheral count recovery around the
bone marrow assessment dates. If the marrow was in morphologic remission and the peripheral
counts made the criteria for CR or CRh in this window, the FDA agreed on a time of response of
the marrow sampling. Second, if the marrow showed blast response without the peripheral
counts meeting criteria for CR or CRh in the -7 to +14 day window but later meeting criteria for
CR or CRh after day 14, the FDA would consider it a response if the marrow was still in
remission at the next bone marrow assessment. The FDA posited that demonstration of bone
marrow remission before and after count recovery could be extrapolated to reasonably assume
that the marrow was in remission at the time of count recovery, had it been assessed on that day.
For these cases, the FDA would assign the time of remission as the time of peripheral count
recovery.

Study Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
The primary efficacy endpoint for Phase 2 was the CR/CRh rate. CR/CRh rate is an accepted
binary efficacy endpoint commonly used to demonstrate direct clinical benefit to support full
approval of treatments for AML in the palliative setting, per FDA guidance for industry (Acute
Myeloid Leukemia: Developing Drugs and Biological Products for Treatment; 2022). This
approach, with demonstration of durability and transfusion independence, was used previously
with FDA-approved targeted inhibitors in R/R acute leukemia (eg, revumenib, olutasidenib,
ivosidenib, enasidenib, gilteritinib).
The response endpoints were defined in accordance with FDA guidance and 2022 ELN
guidelines as shown in

Applicant Table 4. Secondary efficacy endpoints included duration of CR/CRh, transfusion
independence, CR/CRh with MRD negativity, CRc (CR+CRh+CRi), ORR
(CR+CRh+CRi+MLFS+PR), EFS, and OS. Data from these endpoints are presented as
additional evidence of clinical benefit in support of the primary endpoint. The Phase 1b portion

The FDA’s Assessment:

APPEARS
THIS WAY
ON
ORIGINAL

The FDA adjudicated the CR + CRh responses using slightly different definitions than described
in

Applicant Table 4: the FDA used > rather than ≥ for the thresholds for blood recovery counts.
As such, counts were required to be: neutrophil count > 1,000 µL and platelet count > 100,000
µL for CR and neutrophils > 500 µL and platelets > 50,000 µL for CRh. The difference in the
treated patients’ response type and date between the investigator’s assessments and the FDA’s
adjudication is summarized below in Data Quality and Integrity section.
Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The SAP incorporated Agency feedback and the one amendment of the SAP, version 2, was
finalized prior to the database lock for the planned primary analysis.
The SAP defined the following analysis populations for primary efficacy and safety evaluation:
Modified Intent-to-Treat (mITT): all patients who received at least 1 dose of ziftomenib. The
Phase 2 mITT was used for the primary efficacy and safety analysis.
Efficacy Analysis Set (EAS): all patients with locally confirmed NPM1 mutations who received
an initial dose of 600 mg QD ziftomenib. A supportive efficacy analysis was conducted using the
Pooled Population, which includes the Phase 1 Part 1b EAS and Phase 2 mITT analysis set.
Per-Protocol Analysis Set (PPS): all Phase 2 mITT patients who received at least 21 days of
ziftomenib and had no significant protocol deviations that could substantially impact the results
of the primary analysis. A supportive analysis using the PPS was performed for the evaluation of
CR/CRh rate.
The primary efficacy endpoint was CR/CRh rate, a palliative endpoint routinely accepted in this
treatment setting for targeted agents. The Phase 2 sample size of at least 85 patients was chosen
to evaluate the safety profile further and secure adequate power to determine whether the
CR/CRh rate of ziftomenib was greater than the null hypothesis of 0.12 (a CR/CRh rate of 12%
based on overestimated historical data of R/R NPM1-m patients in a clinical trial control arm).
With a 1-sided alpha of 0.025, a total of 85 NPM1-m patients provided 89% power to exclude
the null hypothesis. The estimand for the primary endpoint analysis is detailed in the SAP
Section 7.5.1.
161
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
The primary analysis was triggered when 168 days had passed since the 85th NPM1-m patient
was dosed or when the last patient had completed their safety follow-up period and/or End of
Study. The timing of the primary analysis was set to ensure that at least 85 patients, the planned
sample size, had a minimum of 168 days of follow-up time, which corresponded to the
maximum number of treatment cycles (6 cycles) that could be completed in a 6-month period.
Key secondary endpoints to further support clinical benefit demonstrated by CR/CRh rate
included duration of CR/CRh and transfusion independence. Duration of CR/CRh was defined,
for patients who achieved BOR as CR or CRh, as the time from the first occurrence of CR or
CRh to documented hematological relapse or death due to any cause, whichever occurs first. TI
was defined as the absence of red blood cell (RBC) and platelet transfusions for at least 56
consecutive days in post-baseline period.
Restricted mean survival time (RMST) was added as a post-hoc analysis as an alternative
approach to estimating DOR. The restricted mean survival time for duration of CR, duration of
CRh, duration of CR/CRh, duration of CRc, and duration of ORR have been summarized using
various truncation time points, including 9 months, 12 months, the time point when at least 10%
of patients remained at risk, and the maximum observed duration.
No interim analysis was performed. An interim analysis was originally planned for Phase 2 but
following Agency feedback regarding the size of the overall safety population, it was removed.
The interim analysis was excluded from the SAP before the analysis timepoint and subsequently
removed from the protocol in Amendment 9. All alpha was allocated to the primary analysis.
Rules for the handling of missing data were specified in the SAP Section 5.2 to ensure overall
data integrity.
Factors for planned subgroup analyses included sex, race, region, age, number of prior lines of
therapy, prior HSCT, prior venetoclax exposure, subtypes of NPM1 mutation, baseline ECOG
performance score, baseline extramedullary disease status, and sub-type of AML. Subgroup
analyses of baseline co-mutation status (eg, FLT3, IDH1/2) may also be performed.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant justified the null rate of 12% based on the following related data and literature:
•

The subgroup of patients with NPM1-m AML from the ADMIRAL study comparing
gilteritinib versus investigator choice salvage chemotherapy in R/R AML. Although a
significant proportion of patients in the NPM1 mutated subgroup from the ADMIRAL
study received high intensity chemotherapy, which would be inappropriate for KO-MEN001 patients, the CR + CRh rate of the chemotherapy salvage therapy was 15.3% in
patients either refractory to or relapsing following first-line induction therapy. 7

Perl AE, et al (2019). Gilteritinib or chemotherapy for relapsed or refractory FLT3-mutated AML. New Engl J
Med: 381: 1728-40.

The patients with first relapse or refractory AML from the VALOR study comparing
vosaroxin + cytarabine vs. cytarabine salvage therapy. The CR rate of the cytarabine
salvage therapy was 16.3%. 8
The CR response of patients treated in second salvage with a less intense therapy was
only 4% regardless of the intensity of the first salvage therapy. 9 In patients treated with
venetoclax in combination with hypomethylating agents or low dose cytarabine where the
majority of patients (84%) had received ≥ 2 prior therapies, the CR + CRi response was

Furthermore, despite the higher response rates observed in the R/R setting, patients with
NPM1-m did not retain a favorable prognostic impact as compared to NPM1-wt, and the
probability of achieving a response also declined with each sequential line of salvage
therapy. 11

FDA noted the aforementioned literature sources did not include the same disease population as
the proposed indication of R/R AML with NPM1-m; in particular, for multiple sources, the
reported response rates were provided for the general population of patients with R/R AML
rather than a specific subgroup of patients with NPM1-m. However, with the expectation that the
NPM1 mutation does not represent either a more favorable or unfavorable prognostic factor in
the R/R disease space, these literature sources were considered for contextual purpose to
understand the performance of low intensity chemotherapy in the proposed study population.

the effectiveness in the proposed disease population. This pooled analysis set is referred
to as the Pooled Efficacy Cohort in this review.
o The Phase 2 cohort and Phase 1b cohort were also evaluated separately, as per the
SAP, and are included in the FDA’s review.

Handling of intercurrent events was the same in SAP and this review for primary
analysis:
o Starting new anti-cancer therapy (including HSCT): while on treatment policy
strategy, i.e., response is considered only up to the initiation of new therapy.
o Discontinuation of treatment: treatment policy strategy, i.e., treatment
discontinuation is ignored, and response data are continued to be followed up.
Handling of missing data was the same in SAP and this review for primary analysis:
patients with no post-baseline response assessment were assumed to have not achieved
CR + CRh (composite strategy).
The analysis method was the same in SAP and this review for primary analysis: ClopperPearson exact 95% CI, whose lower bound was compared against the benchmark of 12%.

Regarding DOR, the SAP specified the following censoring rules:
•
•

Starting new anti-cancer therapy (including HSCT): censor at the last assessment date
prior to the initiation of new therapy.
Loss to follow up or withdrawal by patient: censor at the last assessment date prior to
these intercurrent events.

A difference in the censoring rules of DOR with the above specifications is that the FDA
considers HSCT as one standard of care for the study population and therefore would not censor
at the date of receiving HSCT in the primary analysis of DOR.
Regarding the removal of the originally planned interim analysis, the Applicant communicated
this to FDA on 12 January 2023 (IND 142028, SDN 164). However, this was not reflected in the
protocol amendment 8 dated 11 May 2023, but was reflected later in SAP dated 20 September
2023 and protocol amendment 9 dated 13 September 2024. See Applicant Table 5 for more
details.
Regarding the analysis of TI, the baseline period was not explicitly defined in the protocol nor
the SAP. The primary analysis reported in CSR used the time window of 28 days prior to the
first dose of study treatment to 28 days post the first dose or the last dose, whichever was earlier.
FDA did not agree with this unconventional definition for the primary analysis and applied an
alternative window for the primary analysis, which was 28 days prior to the first dose of study
treatment to the first dose.

Table of Demographic Characteristics
Data:
A summary of demographic characteristics is presented in Applicant Table 6. Patients in the
Phase 2 portion of Study KO-MEN-001 (N=92) had a median baseline age of 69.0 (range, 33 to
84) years, with the majority of patients (64.1%) being over the age of 65 years. Most patients
were female (53.3%), White (81.5%), and not Hispanic or Latino (79.3%). Patients were evenly
distributed from sites in the US and Canada (48.9%) and Europe and the United Kingdom
(51.1%). There were no clinically relevant differences between the Phase 2 mITT population and
the supportive Phase 1b EAS population.

A summary of prior antineoplastic therapies in the Phase 2 mITT portion of KO MEN 001 are
presented in Applicant Table 8. Patients reported a median number of prior lines of therapy at
baseline of 2.0 (range 1 to 7), with 58.7% receiving prior venetoclax and 23.9% receiving prior
stem cell transplantation. This baseline number of prior therapies confirms that the Phase 2
patient population, on average, had already failed on at least 2 prior therapies and were starting
on their third or greater line of treatment for their disease. In the Phase 1b patient population, the
median number of prior therapies at baseline was identical to the Phase 2 population, lending
additional confidence to the similarity of these populations for comparison as a supportive
efficacy analysis population.

Treatment compliance was well maintained with high median relative dose intensity.
Concomitant medication use was representative of an AML patient population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The treatment duration of ziftomenib was short with median duration of 2.2 months (range: 0.07,
21.5 months) in the Pooled Efficacy Cohort, with only 21 (18.8%) patients having received
treatment for at least 6 months. These results were similar between the Phase 1 and 2 patients, as
the median of treatment duration was 2.4 (range: 0.5, 14.7) and 2.2 (range: 0.07, 21.5) months.
Clinical TL Comment: Of note, the time required to achieve remission is often delayed when
non-cytotoxic, targeted agents are used for the treatment of R/R AML, as shown in Le, et al. 12 In
this analysis, the median time to remission (CR + CRh) was 2.8 months, and the median time on
treatment was 4.2 months for these agents. Given the median duration of treatment of only 2.2
months in this study, this short treatment duration may affect the observation of the efficacy
endpoints including response rate and durability.
Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses)
Data:
The primary efficacy endpoint in the KO-MEN-001 Phase 2 cohort was CR/CRh rate. At the
protocol-specified planned primary analysis with a data cutoff of 28 Oct 2024, the study met the
primary endpoint with statistical significance, demonstrating a CR/CRh rate of 21.7% (95% CI:
13.8, 31.6; p=0.0058). The CR/CRh rate compared favorably to the null hypothesis of 12% based
on historical data. Results are presented in Applicant Table 9.
Subgroup analysis of CR/CRh rate based on factors such as geographic location, prior therapies,
ECOG score and others aligned with the primary Phase 2 data, showing no clinically meaningful
or statistically significant differences in CR/CRh rates between the predefined subgroups as
evidenced by overlapping error bars compared to the overall CR/CRh results (Applicant Figure
2).
Among 54 patients with prior venetoclax exposure, clinical responses indicated that ziftomenib
maintained activity in patients who relapse after or were refractory to prior venetoclax. The
CR/CRh rate was 22.2% (95% CI: 12.0%, 35.6%) in patients with prior venetoclax exposure
compared with 21.1% (95% CI: 9.6%, 37.3%) in patients without prior venetoclax exposure.
This represents a notable improvement over published responses which suggest venetoclax
failure is associated with poor response to follow-on therapy (Gangat et al, 2023; Maiti et al,
2021). Although there was a trend toward differential response rates by region (North America

Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
Data:
Duration of Response and Time to Response
Duration of Response (DOR) and time to response are summarized in Applicant Table 10. As of
the cutoff date of 28 October 2024, the median follow-up time in the Phase 2 mITT population
was 4.1 months (range: 0.1 to 18.4 months). The data cutoff occurred when the 85th patient
completed 6 cycles. This positive CR/CRh response was achieved in a median time to CR/CRh
response of 2.8 months (95% CI: 1.0, 15.0) and a subsequent median duration of CR/CRh
response of 3.7 months (95% CI: 1.9 to 7.4) for a median period of disease control of greater
than 6 months for CR/CRh. The median time to CR/CRh for patients treated with ziftomenib in
the Phase 2 mITT population is similar to the median time to CR/CRh observed with other
differentiating agents, such as IDH inhibitors (approximately 2.7 months; DiNardo et al, 2018).
This is, in part, because menin inhibitors are not directly cytotoxic, but instead require the
terminal differentiation of leukemic blasts, which are subsequently cleared via scheduled
apoptosis. Therefore, a best response of CR/CRh to ziftomenib is the result of an evolution of the
response over time where the patient has consistent disease control with blast clearance prior to
CR/CRh. In patients who achieved a CR/CRh response, the Kaplan Meier (KM) probability of
maintaining a CR/CRh response to treatment at 3 and 6 months was 57.3% and 33.5%,
respectively, which provides evidence to the durability of response to ziftomenib treatment. Due
to the potential for instability of KM estimates at lower sample sizes or significant censoring, an
ad hoc restricted mean for each of the response durations was performed in accordance with
FDA guidance. The restricted mean duration of CR/CRh response was 4.7 months (95% CI: 2.9,
6.5), validating the duration of response estimates as clinically meaningful.
The median time to achieving a response of CR was 2.8 months (95% CI: 1.8, 3.8) and a median
duration of CR (3.7 months [95% CI: 2.8, 7.4]) similar to those seen with CR/CRh responses in
patients treated with ziftomenib.
The median time to achieve CRc was also 2.8 months (95% CI: 0.8, 4.6) similar to that observed
with the response of CR/CRh, and a median duration of CRc of 4.6 months (95% CI: 2.8, 11.4).
These data suggest a median disease control period of greater than 7 months in patients who
achieve a CRc response.

Table 11. The best overall responses included 13 patients (14.1%) with CR, 7 patients (7.6%)

43.2]) patients and a median duration of overall response of 4.6 months (95% CI: 2.8, 7.4). This
included 2 patients with responses of MLFS and PR prior to HSCT who improved to CR MRDpost-HSCT. Overall 10 of 22 (45.5%) patients, who achieved ORR and had MRD assessed,
achieved MRD-. Patients achieved a response to treatment quickly at a median of 1.9 months
(95% CI: 0.8, 3.7) and maintained that response to ziftomenib treatment for a median duration of
4.6 months (95% CI: 2.8, 7.4), suggesting a median treatment ORR benefit exceeding 6 months
in this patient population with R/R NPM1-m AML.

Transfusion Independence
Conversion to transfusion independent and maintenance of transfusion independence are
summarized in Applicant Table 12 for the Phase 2 mITT population and the supportive Phase 1b
EAS population. In the Phase 2 mITT population, among the 82 patients who were dependent on
RBC and/or platelet transfusions at baseline, 16 (19.5%) patients became transfusion
independent (TD to TI) for both RBC and platelets, and 2 of 10 (20.0%) patients maintained their
TI postbaseline (TI to TI). Further assessment of TI conversion and maintenance rates by RBC
and platelets is provided in Applicant Table 12.
Comparing transfusion conversion and maintenance results to response for either RBC or platelet
transfusions, nearly all patients who were treatment responders achieved TI postbaseline. Of
note, 3 of the 4 patients with a BOR of CRi did not require platelet transfusions for 47 days, 44
days, and 38 days. The Phase 1b results provide supportive evidence of the ziftomenib treatment
effect on transfusion requirements in this patient population, as displayed by the overall pooled
populations. Taken together, these results provide evidence that ziftomenib treatment is
nonmyelosuppressive and provides added clinical benefit beyond treatment response criteria,
such as reduced bleeding risk, decreased fatigue, and maintained independence in a patient’s
daily activities.

Overall Survival
In the Phase 2 portion of Study KO-MEN-001 with a median of 2 prior therapies at baseline, the
median OS was 6.6 months (95% CI: 3.6, 8.6) (Applicant Table 13). In this patient population,
the KM probabilities of OS at 6 or 12 months were estimated to be 53.0% (95% CI: 41.6, 63.1)
and 30.6% (95% CI: 19.8, 42.1), respectively. At 18 months, the OS probability was 28.0%
(95% CI: 17.3, 39.8), suggesting a prolonged survival benefit in over one-quarter of patients in
this R/R NPM1-m AML population. The median OS and KM probabilities observed in the Phase
1b EAS and pooled populations were similar to those seen in the Phase 2 mITT population with
no clinically relevant differences observed.
Although Study KO-MEN-001 is a single-arm Phase 2 study, the 6.6-month median OS
underscores the clinical benefit afforded by ziftomenib monotherapy in R/R NPM1-m AML
patients.

The Applicant’s Position:
The primary efficacy endpoint in the KO-MEN-001 Phase 2 cohort was CR/CRh rate, and the
key secondary endpoint was duration of remission. This positive CR/CRh response was achieved
in a median time to CR/CRh response of 2.8 months (95% CI: 1.0, 15.0) and a subsequent
median duration of CR/CRh response of 3.7 months (95% CI: 1.9 to 7.4) for a median period of
disease control of greater than 6 months for CR/CRh. Responders maintained response for up to
11.4 months. The restricted mean duration of CR/CRh response was 4.7 months (95% CI: 2.9,
6.5), validating the aforementioned duration of response estimates as clinically meaningful.
Patients who achieve CR/CRh are considered to have a clinically meaningful reduction in their
disease status by displaying less than 5% blasts in bone marrow and full recovery of neutrophils
and platelets (or partial recovery, CRh), which reduces infection and bleeding risks, decreases
transfusion-related complications, and improves quality of life. While CRi is not a regulatory
endpoint in this setting, the aforementioned benefits are also seen in patients achieving CRi
(contributing to CRc), as patients with AML and persistent neutropenia are already in a high-risk
category for infection and are recommended to receive antimicrobial prophylaxis carrying their
own inherent risks. The additional secondary endpoints support the primary endpoint with
regards to overall clinical benefit. The CRc (26.1%) and ORR (32.6%) rates further qualify the
clinical importance of the durable CR/CRh rate of 27.1% demonstrated in KO-MEN-001 Phase
2. 16 (19.5%) patients became transfusion independent (TD to TI) for both RBC and platelets,
and 2 of 10 (20.0%) patients maintained their TI postbaseline (TI to TI). The rates of conversion
to (19.5%) and maintenance of (20%) transfusion independence corroborate the primary efficacy

Dose/Dose Response
Data:
The relationship between ziftomenib steady-state exposure and 4 different combinations of
efficacy endpoints was assessed in efficacy E-R analysis, as discussed in Sections 6.2.1and
6.2.2.1. There were no statistically significant correlations between steady state AUC, Cmax or
Ctrough and any efficacy endpoint. Based on these analyses it can be concluded that there is no
exposure-efficacy relationship across the observed exposure range in patients with NPM1-m
AML.
The Applicant’s Position:
The integration of ziftomenib exposure-response findings for both efficacy and safety support the
recommended dose of 600 mg QD dose of ziftomenib as the optimal regimen, maximizing
efficacy in patients with NPM1-m R/R AML while maintaining a favorable safety profile.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Please see Section 6 for a description of the exposure/response analyses.
Durability of Response
Data:
The 21.7% CR/CRh rate was achieved in a median time to CR/CRh response of 2.8 months
(95% CI: 1.0, 15.0) and a subsequent median duration of CR/CRh response of 3.7 months (95%
CI: 1.9 to 7.4) for a median period of disease control of greater than 6 months for CR/CRh (refer
to Applicant Table 9 and Applicant Table 10). Due to the potential for instability of KM
estimates at lower sample sizes or significant censoring, an ad hoc restricted mean for each of the
response durations was performed in accordance with FDA guidance. The restricted mean
duration of CR/CRh response was 4.7 months (95% CI: 2.9, 6.5), validating the aforementioned
duration of response estimates as clinically meaningful.
The positive CR/CRh results are supported by a clinically meaningful median composite
complete remission (CRc) rate (26.1% [95% CI: 17.5, 36.3), which includes patients achieving
CR/CRh and CR with incomplete hematologic recovery (CRi). The median time to CRc response
was 2.8 (95% CI: 0.8, 4.6) months and a median CRc duration of 4.6 (95% CI: 2.8, 11.4)

beyond those achieving CR/CRh alone. The probability of maintaining a CRc response at 6

In addition, ORR provides critical context for the durability of response associated with
ziftomenib treatment. In the Phase 2 mITT population, ORR was achieved in 30 (32.6% [95%
CI: 23.2, 43.2]) patients and a duration of overall response of 4.6 (95% CI: 2.8, 7.4) months, with
similar proportions of patients achieving ORR and durations of ORR in the supportive Phase 1b
EAS population, as described in 8.1.2. Median OS was 6.6 months in patients treated with
ziftomenib, with 12-month and 18-month probabilities of maintaining this response of 30.6% and
28.0%, respectively.
The Applicant’s Position:
The durable CR/CRh rate, supported by other durable measures of clinical benefit (CRc, ORR)
represent a clinically meaningful treatment effect and substantial improvement over historically
available data in this deeply relapsed, refractory population otherwise relegated to cytotoxic
treatment options in the palliative setting.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Among the 24 participants who achieved CR or CRh on treatment, 17 participants had disease
that either progressed or relapsed, 1 died, 1 started subsequent anti-cancer therapy and relapsed
after this initiation, 2 discontinued treatment and withdrew from study without disease
progression or relapse, and 3 remained in remission at the time of the data cutoff date of 21
March 2025. Among the 3 participants who had ongoing response, none received HSCT after the
ziftomenib treatment. The review team notes that comparison to historical controls is difficult
due to potential confounding variables, including differences in patient and disease
characteristics. As such, any comparison to data outside of the trial should be interpreted with
caution.

Integrated Review of Effectiveness
Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
The Applicant’s Position:
Clinical efficacy was assessed using data and analysis from Study KO-MEN-001, there were no
additional trials for the assessment of clinical efficacy that were integrated. The data from
KO-MEN-001 Phase 2 is a single adequate, well-controlled trial that provides the primary
efficacy analysis to support the proposed indication. The Phase 1b results provide supportive
evidence. The results for Phase 1b are presented alongside the primary analysis population in
Section 8.1.2.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Methods
The Applicant proposed an indication “for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or
refractory acute myeloid leukemia (AML) with an NPM1 mutation,” with a dose of ziftomenib
600 mg PO daily.
Issues with the clinical development program:
The clinical development program included one multiple-cohort, single-arm, Phase 1-2 trial of
ziftomenib monotherapy (KO-MEN-001) in adult patients ≥ 18 years of age with relapsed or
refractory acute myeloid leukemia with an NPM1 mutation by local testing. The Phase 1a portion
was an open-label, dose-escalation study with 7 cohorts and included multiple genetic subtypes
of R/R acute leukemia. The Phase 1b portion treated participants with R/R NPM1-m AML with
200 mg daily (n=17) or 600 mg daily (n=36) of ziftomenib. The Phase 2 portion included a
194
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
single cohort of participants treated with ziftomenib 600 mg daily, designed to target a CR +
CRh rate of 25% and exclude a 12% null rate.
•

A major limitation in the developmental program is that only one adequate and wellcontrolled study was conducted to support this application, and the study was a singlearm, non-randomized study. In general, for a single pivotal study to support an NDA, the
trial should be well-designed, well-conducted, and provide statistically persuasive
efficacy findings that would make a second study ethically or practically impossible to
perform. In addition, additional confirmatory evidence should be provided. The
Applicant proposed to use the Phase 1b cohort treated at the RP2D as the supporting
evidence of effectiveness in addition to the Phase 2 pivotal cohort. However, the final
adjudicated responses did not exclude the 12% null rate from the pivotal cohort. As such,
the labeled population includes the Pooled Efficacy Cohort of Phase 1b + Phase 2.

Issues with the pivotal trial:
The review team identified several issues with the design and conduct of the trial:
•

•

•

•

The protocol and SAP did not specify a window between the marrow sampling and the
CBC for remission assessment. This complicated the independent response adjudication
based on the raw data. For the adjudication process, the FDA used a window of (-7, +14)
days in addition to with clinical judgement. While the results submitted by the Applicant
for the Phase 2 cohort excluded the benchmark rate of 12%, the adjudicated results of the
Phase 2 cohort did not. This raises questions about the robustness of the results.
Although the CR + CRh rate in the Phase 2 patients excluded the null hypothesis of 12%
based on the Applicant’s assessments, it did not based on the FDA’s adjudication. The
FDA also evaluated this in the Pooled Efficacy Cohort by pooling the Phase 1 patients
given two phases had the same eligibility criteria and two phases of patients had similar
baseline characteristic. This differed from what was specified in the SAP but provided a
more comprehensive experience. The efficacy results from the Pooled Efficacy Cohort
appear to confirm the initial inference based on the Applicant’s assessments for Phase 2
patients only. The FDA evaluated the impact of borrowing from Phase 1 data in
sensitivity analyses, concluding that the inference was generally similar even when the
Phase 1 data were only partially used for pooling.
Remission is generally delayed in onset for other molecularly targeted agents for
treatment of AML compared to traditional cytotoxic therapy. For the Pooled Efficacy
Cohort in Study KO-MEN-001, the median time on treatment was only 9.6 weeks. How
this short follow-up time affected observation of the efficacy endpoints needs to be
considered.
Of note is the low proportion of patients achieving CR + CRh, the low rate of conversion
to transfusion independence and the low rate of maintenance of transfusion
independence. These observations may be explained in part by the short duration of

Supporting Endpoints
See Section 8.1.2 for the analyses of supporting endpoints, including time to response,
achievement of transfusion independence (red blood cell and platelet), and overall survival (OS).
The results for time to response and conversion to TI are discussed above. As OS is not
interpretable in a single-arm trial, it is not considered further here.
Dose/Dose Response
Assessment of monotherapy dose response is challenging due to the limited number of
participants with NPM1-m AML treated at doses other than the recommended dosage of 600 mg
daily during the dose escalation phase of KO-MEN-001. Only 8 participants with NPM1-m AML
were treated at other doses: 6 at 200 mg daily, 1 at 400 mg daily, and 1 at 800 mg daily. Of these
8 patients, only one (1/6, 17%) participant treated at 200 mg daily was reported to have had a
response of CR. Given the small number of participants treated at this dosage, it is unclear if this
is the true rate of response at the 200 mg daily dosage or if it represents an unexpected response
as this lower dose. It is interesting to note that during the dose escalation phase of development,
more participants were enrolled with KMT2A-r acute leukemia at alternative dose levels, which
ranged from 50 to 1000 mg daily. Of the two dose levels with the highest enrollment of
participants with KMT2A-r AL, the Applicant saw no responses at the 200 mg dose level (0/14,
0%) and 2 responses (2/18, 11%) in those treated at the 600 mg dose level, suggesting a dose
response for ziftomenib in this population.

Study KO-MEN-001 allowed patients to resume treatment of ziftomenib after transplantation if
the following conditions were met:

Must not have active GVHD.
Maximum of 0.5 mg/kg of prednisone equivalent.
Permitted to be on stable doses of calcineurin inhibitors.
AML in remission.

benefit and risk profile of such posttransplant resumption of treatment was not possible to

study in which the CR rates were only 2% and CRc rates were 4% (Perl et al, 2019). The median
time to CR response was 2.3 months (95% CI: 0.9, 7.4) with a median duration of CR of 5.6
months (95% CI: 0.6, 8.1) for a median period of disease control of approximately 8 months in

majority (64.3% [95% CI: 35.1, 87.2]) of responding patients achieving MRD- on local readings

Improvements in platelet count are associated with decreased transfusion burden and lower risk
of related complications. Among the 82 patients who were dependent on RBC and/or platelet
transfusions at baseline, 16 (19.5%) patients experienced conversion from TD to transfusion
independent (TI) for both RBC and platelets and 2 of 10 (20.0%) patients maintained their TI (TI
to TI) for a 56-day postbaseline period. Ziftomenib has been demonstrated to be a nontoxic and
nonmyelosuppressive treatment, rendering TI an applicable measure of direct clinical benefit
corroborating the clinical endpoint of CR/CRh.
The data from KO-MEN-001 Phase 2 provide is a single adequate, well-controlled trial that
provides the primary efficacy analysis to support the proposed indication to support the proposed
indication, with Phase 1b providing supportive evidence. The results for Phase 1b are presented
with the primary analysis population in Section 8.1.2. The totality of efficacy data provide
additional supportive evidence of a clinically meaningful and durable improvement in disease
control with ziftomenib in R/R NPM1-m AML, which remains difficult to treat this setting and
for which no FDA-approved targeted therapies exist.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The efficacy of ziftomenib was established based on the results of the Phase 2 pivotal cohort in
Study KO-MEN-001 in adult participants with R/R AML with an NPM1 mutation, with
confirmatory data from the Phase 1b cohort of participants with NPM1-m AML treated at the
recommended dosage of 600 mg daily. The demographics of the study population were
consistent with those of the US population with NPM1-m AML, and the results were considered
generalizable to the US population (see Section 8.1.1).
In Study KO-MEN-001, eligible participants who received ziftomenib at the recommended
dosage had a CR/CRh rate of 21.4% (95% CI: 14.2%, 30.2%) in the Pooled Efficacy Cohort and
18.5% (95% CI: 11.1%, 27.9%) in its Phase 2 subset (FDA Table 37). The median duration of
response was 5.0 (95% CI: 1.9, 8.1) months, and the median time to response was 2.7 (range: 0.9
to 15) months in the Pooled Efficacy Cohort. The rate of conversion to transfusion independence
was 21.2% (95% CI: 12.1%, 33.0%) and transfusion maintenance rate was 26.1% (95%
CI:14.3%, 41.1%) in the Pooled Efficacy Cohort.
The relatively low CR/CRh rate, low TI rate, and short follow-up limiting assessment of
durability leads to some uncertainty about clinical benefit. However, the results from the Phase 2
efficacy cohort of Study KO-MEN-001 and the additional confirmatory data from the Phase 1b
portion of KO-MEN-001 are considered substantial evidence of effectiveness of ziftomenib for

Review of Safety
THE APPLICANT’S POSITION
The pivotal study, KO-MEN-001 (main study), was the primary source of data for the evaluation
of the safety of 600 mg QD of ziftomenib as a monotherapy for the treatment of patients with
R/R NPM1-m AML. The primary safety population included all patients with a confirmed NPM1
mutation who received 600 mg QD, including the 92 patients in Phase 2 and 20 patients in Phase
1b for a total of 112 patients.
Additional supportive sources of safety data summarized within the application include other
ongoing clinical studies of ziftomenib. The KO-MEN-001 sub-studies are evaluating drug-drug
interactions in non-NPM1-m AML (sub-studies 1 and 2) or ziftomenib activity in ALL (substudy 3), and Studies KO-MEN-007 and KO-MEN-008 evaluate ziftomenib in combination with
standard-of- care regimens (eg, venetoclax, azacitidine, intensive and low-dose chemotherapy,
and gilteritinib). As these data do not directly inform the proposed indication of patients with
R/R NPM1-m AML due to the inherent heterogeneity of combination agents, molecular types
and study populations, summary-level data are presented in the application for a comprehensive
package without integration. For studies evaluating the combination of ziftomenib with standardof-care regimens (eg, KO-MEN-007 and KO-MEN-008), safety data will be summarized for
each individual cohort within each study.
There were also two completed (KO-MEN-003, KO-MEN-005) and one terminated (KO-MEN006) PK studies in healthy volunteers. Safety data from these studies are presented individually
in the respective CSRs and were not included in the ISS.
Safety Review Approach
Data:
The pivotal study, Study KO-MEN-001, was the primary source for the evaluation of the safety
of ziftomenib 600 mg QD as monotherapy for the treatment of patients with R/R NPM1-m AML
(N=112).
In the narrative portion of the ISS, safety information presentation was organized into 3
population groups, as agreed at the Type C Safety Analysis Strategy meeting, with the focus
being on the population supporting the target indication and all non-indication analyses were
presented in the NDA for completeness but considered supplementary (Applicant Table 14).

The Applicant’s Position:
Pivotal Study KO-MEN-001 (main study) will be the primary source for the evaluation of the
safety of ziftomenib for the treatment of patients with R/R NPM1-m AML in this NDA. Safety
data from other supportive studies, including doses of ziftomenib other than 600 mg QD, nonNPM1-m molecular types of AML, or combination regimens do not directly inform the safety
profile of the proposed indication.
Review of the Safety Database
Overall Exposure
Data:
Overall, the median number of doses received was 61.0 (range: 2 to 509). The median duration
of exposure was 9.64 weeks (range: 0.3 to 72.9 weeks). The median cumulative dose was
36,600.0 mg (range: 1200 to 269,600 mg), the median dose intensity was 600.0 mg/day (range:
217.0 to 633.0 mg/day), and the median RDI was 100.0% (range: 36.2% to 105.5%; Applicant
Table 15).

The Applicant’s Position:
The 600 mg QD Monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML Safety Analysis Population includes R/R
AML patients with locally confirmed NPM1-m enrolled in KO-MEN-001 main study, which is
composed of the Part 1b Efficacy Analysis Set (EAS) and Phase 2 modified Intent‑to‑Treat
Analysis Set, who have received an initial dose of 600 mg QD of ziftomenib. The exposure to
ziftomenib observed in the populations studied in KO-MEN-001 is appropriate for the proposed
indication population. No clinically relevant differences in exposure were observed in the other
supportive patient populations presented in Module 2.7.4/ISS.
Relevant characteristics of the safety population:
Data:
Patient demographics were previously presented in Section 8.1.2. Overall, the median age of
patients was 69.0 years (range: 22 to 86 years) and 56.3% of patients were female. Most patients
(56.3%) had an Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) Performance Status score of 1.
Patients had median baseline bone marrow blasts of 44.0% (range: 0.5% to 98%). Most patients
had de novo AML (95 patients [84.8%]). Patients had median prior lines of therapy of 2.0
(range: 1 to 7).
The Applicant’s Position:
The demographics of the population are indicative of the population of patients for the proposed
indication. The supportive Phase 1b population patient demographics were similar to those seen
in patients in Phase 2, with any numerical differences being not clinically relevant.
Adequacy of the safety database:

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
The safety analysis strategy was agreed in the May 2024 Type C written responses. The content
of this clinical package was discussed and considered adequate at the February 2025 Pre-NDA
meeting, as was the content and timing of the safety update.
Phase 2 was designed for registrational intent, with a final total enrollment of 92 patients, to
ensure a robust safety database for patients at the intended registrational dose and population of
600 mg in R/R NPM1-m AML patients and to assess efficacy. The data cutoff of 28 Oct 2024
provides a minimum of 6 cycles of follow-up from the start of treatment for the prespecified
Phase 2 population. Patients with R/R NPM1-m AML and treated with ziftomenib 600 mg QD
were enrolled with the same eligibility criteria, treated with the same protocol, and demonstrated
consistent demographics, safety, and efficacy as the Phase 2 population. The addition of the 20
Phase 1b patients to the primary analysis population of 92 Phase 2 patients, for a total of 112
patients with R/R NPM1-m AML treated at the proposed ziftomenib dose of 600 mg QD,
provides an adequate sample to assess the safety of ziftomenib.
Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
The Applicant’s Position:
No concerns have been identified in the quality and integrity of the submitted datasets and
individual case narratives.
Categorization of Adverse Event
The Applicant’s Position:
All adverse events (AEs) recorded in the electronic case report forms were coded using the
Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA; version 27.0) and graded using
Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (CTCAE; version 5.0).
TEAEs were summarized for the initial treatment-emergent period, the maintenance
treatment‑emergent period after on-treatment HSCT (only for those who had maintenance
ziftomenib treatment after HSCT), and the maintenance treatment-emergent period without
HSCT (only for those who had maintenance ziftomenib treatment without HSCT), separately.
Patient incidence of TEAEs was tabulated by system organ class (SOC) and preferred term (PT).
Selected TEAEs were tabulated by PT in descending order of frequency. Similar summaries
were repeated for AEs of special interest of differentiation syndrome and tumor lysis syndrome.

sedimentation rate
Safety Results

Deaths
Data:
Overall, of the 71 (63.4%) patients who discontinued the study due to death, 27 (24.1%) patients
who experienced a TEAE that lead to death during Study KO MEN 001 in the 600 mg QD
monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML safety population (N=112), with the most common events
being sepsis (5 patients [4.5%]), pneumonia (3 patients [2.7%]), respiratory failure (3 patients
[2.7%]), respiratory distress and cardiac arrest (2 patients [1.8%] each). No TEAEs leading to
death were considered related to ziftomenib treatment. All other events were single reports.
The Applicant’s Position:
R/R NPM1-m AML is a is a serious, progressive disease that is nearly universally fatal. Due to
events attributable to underlying disease progression, on-study deaths are expected in this
population. Overall in the indication population, approximately 25% of subjects reported a
TEAE that lead to death with the main PTs being sepsis, pneumonia, and respiratory failure.
Notably, no cases of differentiation syndrome led to death in this population of patients with R/R
NPM1-m AML. None of these TEAEs leading to death were considered related to ziftomenib
treatment.

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
A total 89 patients (79.5%) in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population
experienced at least 1 serious adverse event (SAE) during ziftomenib treatment (Applicant Table
16), with the events of febrile neutropenia, DS, sepsis, and pneumonia being the most commonly
reported. In this population, 30 patients (26.8%) experienced an SAE that was considered related
to ziftomenib treatment, with the majority of these being DS (16 patients [14.3%]) and diarrhea,
dyspnea, febrile neutropenia, and white blood cell count increased (each in 2 patients [1.8%]).

The Applicant’s Position:
R/R NPM1-m AML is a is a serious, progressive disease that is nearly universally fatal.
Considering events attributable to the underlying progressive disease, the incidence of SAEs
reported in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population is expected in this
patient population. Most patients reported at least 1 SAE with key events of febrile neutropenia,
differentiation syndrome, sepsis, and pneumonia. There were 30 (26.8%) patients who
experienced an SAE deemed related to ziftomenib treatment with the only related events
reported in ≥ 5% of patients being differentiation syndrome, which was demonstrated to be
manageable. Discussion on DS are provided in Section 8.3.5.
Discontinuations, Interruptions, or Dose Reductions Due to Adverse Effects
Data:
207

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
In the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population, 24 patients (21.4%)
experienced at least 1 TEAE that lead to ziftomenib discontinuation, with the events of sepsis (4
patients [3.6%]) and pneumonia (3 patients [2.7%]) being the most common events (Applicant
Table 17). There were 3 patients (2.7%) who experienced a ziftomenib-related TEAE that led to
treatment discontinuation, with the events of DS (2 patients [1.8%]) and vomiting (1 patient
[0.9%]).
A total of 60 patients (53.6%) experienced a TEAE that lead to a ziftomenib dose interruption,
with the most common events being DS (15 patients [13.4%]), sepsis (9 patients [8.0%]), and
febrile neutropenia (6 patients [5.4%]) (Table 18). There were 34 patients (30.4%) who
experienced a ziftomenib-related TEAE that led to a ziftomenib dose interruption, with the most
common PT of DS (15 patients [13.4%]). All other reports of ziftomenib-related TEAEs leading
to dose interruption in >1 patient were white blood cell count increased (3 patients [2.7%]) and
leukocytosis, diarrhea, and pruritus (2 patients [1.8%] each). A further discussion on DS and the
DS mitigation guidance are provided in Section 8.3.5.
There were few patients (4 [3.6%]) who experienced a TEAE that lead to ziftomenib dose
reduction in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population, all of which were
single reports of the PTs of neutropenia, pericarditis, ECG QT prolonged, and DS. The TEAEs
of neutropenia, ECG QT prolonged, and DS were assessed as related to ziftomenib.
