The Applicant’s Position:
Few patients had a TEAE that led to discontinuation or dose reduction. While differentiation
syndrome was the most frequently reported TEAE that led to dose interruption, importantly,
these data demonstrate that DS was manageable with the guidance provided in the protocol,
consistent with proposed labeling, which instructed treatment interruption upon first suspicion
and initiation of systemic corticosteroids, followed by resuming ziftomenib at the same dose
level when DS signs or symptoms improved to Grade 2 or lower. Following implementation of
this DS management guidance, no additional Grade 4 or 5 events were reported in either
genotype. Additional details on DS are provided in Section 8.3.5.

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
A majority of patients experienced at least 1 CTCAE Grade ≥3 TEAE primarily related to
underlying disease. The most common TEAEs of CTCAE Grade ≥3 were febrile neutropenia,
anaemia, and thrombocytopenia. Notably, the adverse reaction of DS was reported as a ≥ Grade
3 TEAE in 15 (13.4%); all cases were Grade 3 with no Grade 4 or 5 events of DS. A discussion
on DS events is presented in Section 8.3.5.
Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
Data:
Overall, in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population, all patients
experienced at least 1 TEAE during ziftomenib treatment with the majority of patients
experiencing a TEAE with a Grade ≥3 (93.8%) and/or a serious TEAE (79.5%). Similarly, most
patients (68.8%) experienced at least 1 ziftomenib-related TEAE during treatment in this
population (Applicant Table 19).

in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population, diarrhea, nausea, hypokalemia,
DS, and pruritus were the most common (Applicant Table 20).

The Applicant’s Position:
The data from the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML safety analysis population,
supported by the data from the overall integrated safety analysis, demonstrates that the safety
profile of continuous 600 mg QD ziftomenib administration for the treatment of NPM1-m AML
patients meets the criteria of a palliative therapy by being relatively nontoxic and
nonmyelosuppressive (FDA, 2022). The reported TEAEs were largely consistent with those
associated with underlying AML and consistent with the predictable safety profile of ziftomenib,
with ziftomenib displaying similar tolerability to other targeted agents approved for use as
palliative therapies.
Laboratory Findings
Data:
Hematology
In patients who continued therapy, increases were observed in mean (SD) hemoglobin levels
(93.13 [15.466] g/L), absolute neutrophil count (2.16 [2.792] × 10⁹/L), and platelets (93.20
[87.315] × 10⁹/L) by Cycle 4 Day 1 that persisted until the EOT or relapse.
Hematology assessments for the 600 mg QD Monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML Safety Analysis
Population are detailed in Table 14.3.4.2.4 (summary of hematology tests by visit) and Table
14.3.4.5.4 (shift of CTCAE grade in hematology laboratory tests) of the KO MEN 001 CSR.
Biochemistry
In patients who continued therapy, albumin levels increased, and lactate dehydrogenase levels
declined with longer treatment duration, consistent with response and overall clinical
improvement.
These changes occurred in the absence of hepatic, renal, or electrolyte changes.
Biochemistry assessments for the 600 mg QD Monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML Safety Analysis
Population are detailed in Table 14.3.4.3.4 (summary of biochemistry tests by visit) and Table
14.3.4.6.4 (shift of CTCAE grade in biochemistry laboratory tests) of the KO MEN 001 CSR.
Coagulation
In patients who continued therapy, improvements in D-dimer, international normalized ratio, and
activated partial thromboplastin time were observed with prolonged treatment, consistent with
response.
Coagulation assessments for the 600 mg QD Monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML Safety Analysis
Population are detailed in Table 14.3.4.4.4 (summary of coagulation tests by visit) and Table
14.3.4.7.4 (shift of CTCAE grade in coagulation laboratory tests) of the KO MEN 001.

Electrocardiograms (ECGs) / QT
Data:
In the 600 mg QD Monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population, TEAEs of electrocardiogram
QT prolonged (12 patients [10.7%]), atrial fibrillation (3 patients [2.7%]), bundle branch block
(1 patient [0.9%]), electrocardiogram T wave abnormal (1 patient [0.9%]), and ventricular
tachycardia (1 patient [0.9%]) were reported. Of these, events of electrocardiogram QT
prolonged in 3 patients (2.7%) and bundle branch block in 1 patient (0.9%) were assessed as
related to ziftomenib. Review of the patients with ziftomenib-related events of electrocardiogram
QT prolonged (Grade 2=1 patient, Grade 3=2 patients) identified that all 3 patients were on
multiple concomitant QT prolonging medications; 2 patients had either hypokalemia or
hypocalcemia at the time of the event, and the third patient had a medical history of atrial
fibrillation. There were no ECG‑related SAEs. TEAEs of electrocardiogram QT prolonged in 4
patients (3.6%) and a ziftomenib-related TEAE of bundle branch block in 1 patient (0.9%) led to
ziftomenib dose interruption. A ziftomenib-related TEAE of electrocardiogram QT prolonged in
1 patient (0.9%) led to ziftomenib dose reduction. There were no ECG-related TEAEs that led to
ziftomenib discontinuation. Refer to Module 2.7.4, Section 4.1.1 (narrative portion of ISS).
Concentration-corrected QT interval (QTc), by time point, categorical and morphological
analyses of ECGs from 31 patients in KO-MEN-001 Parts 1a and 1b were conducted to assess
the cardiodynamic effects of ziftomenib (KO-PK-0003).
217
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
The relationship between baseline-corrected QTcF (ΔQTcF) and plasma concentrations of
ziftomenib (and metabolites KO-516 and KO-739) were investigated using a linear mixed-effects
modeling approach. The full model included ΔQTcF as the dependent variable, plasma
concentration of ziftomenib as the explanatory variables, centered baseline QTcF (ie, baseline
QTcF for individual patient minus the population mean baseline QTcF for all patients) as an
additional covariate, a fixed intercept (ie, a population mean intercept), and random effects on
intercept and slopes per patient. The model-predicted effect and its 2-sided 90% confidence
interval (CI) for ΔQTcF (ie, the sum of slope estimate × concentration for all included analytes +
population mean intercept) at these sets of concentrations were obtained. The predictions were
based on maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Cycle 2 Day 1. If the
upper bound of the 2-sided 90% CI (equivalent to the upper bound of the 1-sided 95% CI) of the
model-predicted QTc effect (ΔQTcF) was below 10 ms at clinically relevant plasma levels, it
could be further concluded that ziftomenib did not cause clinically relevant QTc prolongation
within their observed plasma concentration ranges. Based on this concentration-QTc analysis, an
effect on ∆QTcF with 90% upper confidence interval (UCI) exceeding 10 ms can be predicted
for ziftomenib plasma concentrations of approximately 865 ng/mL, and with 90% UCI
exceeding 20 ms can be expected at approximately 1380 ng/mL (Applicant Figure 3). At steady
state, ziftomenib mean Cmax is 881 ng/mL at 600 mg QD, thus demonstrating that ziftomenib had
no clinically significant effect on QTc by excluding an increase of >20 ms at the 600-mg dose
level. Categorical analysis demonstrated that there were no QTcF prolongation of >480 ms or
>500 ms or ΔQTcF >60 ms. Additionally, ziftomenib had no clinically significant effect on heart
rate or on cardiac conduction (ie, the PR and QRS intervals).

Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
8.3.5.1.

Differentiation Syndrome

The Applicant’s Position:
Preclinical PK/pharmacodynamic studies revealed that ziftomenib induces terminal
differentiation and apoptosis, mechanisms that can manifest as DS in clinical settings. As
expected, ziftomenib-related DS was observed throughout all phases of Study KO-MEN-001.
Consistent with other targeted agents for the treatment of AML, differentiation syndrome
emerged as a risk in Study KO-MEN-001 (Phase 1 Part 1a) in 1 patient with R/R KMT2A-r AML
in the 1000 mg cohort experienced Grade 4 DS and subsequently died secondarily due to cardiac
arrest. In Phase 1 Part 1b of Study KO-MEN-001, 17 patients (32.1%; predominantly KMT2A-r)
experienced ziftomenib-related DS, including a Grade 5 (fatal) event in a patient with R/R
KMT2A-r AML at the 200 mg dose level. Following the Grade 5 event, the DS mitigation
guidance was issued, and enrollment of patients with R/R KMT2A-r AML was eventually closed.
A DS mitigation guidance was subsequently developed and included additional monitoring on
Days 4 and 10 for evidence of rising white blood cell count with peripheral evidence of myeloid
differentiation as well as coagulation and inflammatory markers. Monitoring for specific
symptoms that may be possible signs of DS was detailed. Finally, sites were instructed to
proceed with early ziftomenib interruption and institution of steroid therapy at the time of
suspicion of a DS event. Following implementation of this DS guidance, no additional Grade 4
or 5 events were reported in either genotype.
A DS identification algorithm based on Montesinos 2009 and updated with Norsworthy 2020
was developed. A DS dataset encompassing all confirmed DS and potential DS cases (identified
using the algorithm based on Norsworthy 2020) is included as ‘adrsadj’ in the KO-MEN-001
study folder and discussed in the KO-MEN-001 CSR.
Overall, in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML safety analysis population,
27 patients (24.1%) experienced a TEAE of DS, with a median time to the first event of DS
being 15.0 days (range: 3 to 46 days). There were 16 patients (14.3%) who experienced an SAE
of DS, of which all were considered related to ziftomenib treatment. Fifteen patients (13.4%)
experienced an event of DS of a CTCAE Grade 3, all of which were considered related to
ziftomenib treatment. The DS mitigation plan outlined in Study KO-MEN-001 included
immediate treatment interruption for a suspected DS event; there were 15 (13.4%) patients who
experienced a TEAE of DS that lead to a ziftomenib dose interruption. Although these DS events
were considered serious, most cases were manageable per the DS mitigation guidance, with only
2 (1.8%) patients discontinuing ziftomenib treatment due to DS, 1 patient (0.9%) experiencing an
event of DS that led to a dose reduction, and no patients having a CTCAE Grade 4 or 5 DS
event. No patients in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML safety analysis
population experienced TEAEs of DS that led to death.
220
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation

Komzifti (ziftomenib)
These events are consistent with other targeted AML agents. The Sponsor proposes language in
the non-REMs medication guide and specific label language denoting the risks and procedures
related to potential cases of DS arising during ziftomenib treatment.
8.3.5.2.

Tumor Lysis Syndrome

The Applicant’s Position:
Given the nonclinical activity and tumor regression pattern of ziftomenib in multiple tumor
models in rodents, tumor lysis syndrome was a potential risk during the development of
ziftomenib. Among patients in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML safety analysis
population, 2 patients (1.8%) experienced a TEAE of TLS, with none of these events being
considered related to ziftomenib treatment. Both events of TLS were of a CTCAE Grade 3, and 1
of the events was considered an SAE. None of the events lead to dose reduction, interruption, or
discontinuation of ziftomenib treatment. The review of TLS events did not indicate a relationship
between ziftomenib treatment and TLS development.
8.3.5.3.

Drug-Induced Liver Injury

Data:
Based on findings in preclinical toxicology studies suggestive of adverse effects on the
hepatobiliary system, drug-induced liver injury was a potential risk during the development of
ziftomenib. Hy’s law cases were defined as concurrent (within 7 days) AST or ALT >3 × upper
limit of normal (ULN) and total bilirubin >2 × ULN (and ALP <2 × ULN or missing). In the 600
mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population, there were 2 patients (1.8%) who met
potential Hy’s law criteria (Applicant Table 23). Each patient had pre-existing, comorbid
conditions prior to initiation of ziftomenib (bilirubin excretion disorder, blood ALP increased,
blood bilirubin increased, and AST increased) and concomitant SAEs (cholecystitis and
Escherichia sepsis), which, therefore, ruled out an event of Hy’s law. Overall, observed hepatic
changes were generally mild to moderate and well managed according to Study KO MEN 001
procedures.

8.3.5.4.

Bleeding Events

The Applicant’s Position:
Bleeding events are frequently seen in patients with active AML due to underlying marrow
dysfunction with associated thrombocytopenia. More common bleeding events, such as epistaxis,
have been reported in 15.1% of patients receiving ivosidenib (DiNardo et al, 2018), 17.1% of
patients receiving gilteritinib (Perl et al, 2019), and 26.6% of patients receiving revumenib
monotherapy (Issa et al, 2023).
In the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population, there were no CTCAE Grade 4
or 5 bleeding events. There were 14 patients (12.5%) who reported a TEAE of epistaxis, with 2
(1.8%) patients reporting a CTCAE Grade 3 event and none being considered related to
ziftomenib treatment. There were 4 patients (3.6%) who experienced a Grade 3 lower
222
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
gastrointestinal hemorrhage, which was ziftomenib-related in 1 patient (0.9%), and 1 patient
(0.9%) had a Grade 3 subdural hematoma, which was assessed as not related to ziftomenib.
Thrombotic events occur in about 8% to 9% of patients with newly diagnosed nonacute
promyelocytic leukemia (APL; Libourel et al, 2016). Data on disseminated intravascular
coagulation (DIC) in non-APL AML are fewer, but prevalence suggests 8.5% to 25% at
presentation and further events with the commencement of chemotherapy (Ten Cate and Leader,
2021, Uchiumi et al, 2007). Overall, DIC was rare occurring in 4 patients (3.6%) in the 600 mg
QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML population, with the event in 1 patient (0.9%) being
considered related to ziftomenib treatment. A Grade 3 event of pulmonary embolism was
reported in 1 patient (0.9%) in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1-m R/R AML safety analysis
population and was assessed as not related to ziftomenib. Deep vein thrombosis was reported in
3 patients in the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1‑m R/R AML population, and all were assessed
as not related to ziftomenib.
Taken together, these data support that ziftomenib monotherapy does not increase bleeding and
thrombotic events over what is observed in an AML population.
8.3.5.5.

Febrile Neutropenia and Infection

The Applicant’s Position:
Neutropenic fever occurs in up to 80% of patients receiving chemotherapy for hematological
malignancies, including AML, with a risk of major complications of 25% to 30% and fatality
rates of 11%, increasing up to 50% when accompanied with sepsis and/or septic shock (Taplitz et
al, 2018). In the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1‑m R/R AML population, febrile neutropenia
was reported in 25 patients (22.3%) and 20 patients (17.9%) had events that were considered
serious. Three patients (2.7%) experienced an event of febrile neutropenia that was considered
related to ziftomenib treatment.
Due to neutropenia and being immunocompromised from both disease and treatment, infectious
complications are common in AML patients. Pneumonia is one of the more common events
occurring in AML patients, reported in 37% of AML patients receiving palliative therapy and
13.7% of patients receiving induction therapy (Tober et al, 2023). For patients receiving
monotherapy with gilteritinib or ivosidenib, pneumonia was reported in 17.5% and 14.7% of
patients, respectively, with Grade ≥3 pneumonia occurring in 11.8% and 11.6% of patients,
respectively (Perl et al, 2019, DiNardo et al, 2018). In front-line combination therapy with ven
aza, 23% of patients developed pneumonia, including 20% with Grade ≥3 (DiNardo et al, 2018).
In the 600 mg QD monotherapy NPM1‑m R/R AML population, 24 patients (21.4%) reported an
event of pneumonia, with 17 (15.2%) patients having of an event of a CTCAE Grade ≥3 and 12
patients (10.7%) reporting an SAE of pneumonia. None of the events of pneumonia were
considered related to ziftomenib treatment. Additional details are provided in Section 2.1.9.3 of
Module 2.7.4 (narrative portion of ISS).

Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
Overall, the results are consistent with a patient population receiving therapy for AML and there
is no additive infection risk with ziftomenib treatment.
Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable, there were no patient-reported clinical outcome assessments collected.
Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
The Applicant’s Position:
No subgroup analysis of the integrated safety data was performed. Population PK modeling was
used to evaluate the impact of intrinsic factors on ziftomenib PK. Disease state was identified as
a statistically significant covariate. However, as ziftomenib dose optimization was conducted in
patients with R/R NPM1-m AML, no additional dose adjustment was necessary. Based on the
population PK analysis, age, body weight, height, body mass index, body surface area, estimated
glomerular filtration rate, serum creatinine, bilirubin, albumin, ALP, ALT, AST, sex, race,
ethnicity, mutation type, renal impairment (mild or moderate), or hepatic impairment (mild or
moderate) did not affect the PK of ziftomenib. Hence, no dose adjustment is required for any of
the intrinsic factors.
Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable; no safety specific studies were conducted.
Additional Safety Explorations
Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable; no human carcinogenicity or tumor development studies were conducted.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Data:
There are no data available in pregnant women exposed to ziftomenib to assess the risks of
ziftomenib treatment on pregnancy in humans and no pregnancies have been reported in
ziftomenib clinical studies at the time of data cutoff.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
Based on findings from the embryo-fetal toxicity study in mice, ziftomenib is considered
teratogenic and caused adverse developmental outcomes in mice at a level that was 3 times the
proposed human dose exposure level of 600 mg QD. Administration of ziftomenib was not
associated with maternal toxicity at any dose level (KO-TOX-0018). Ziftomenib was neither
mutagenic nor clastogenic/aneugenic in standard in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity assays.
The Applicant’s Position:
No pregnancies have been reported in the ziftomenib clinical studies to date. Ziftomenib may
cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman. Considering the 5 half-lives of
ziftomenib, it is advised that female of reproductive potential and males with partners of
reproductive potential use effective contraception during treatment and for 2 weeks after the last
dose.
Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable; ziftomenib has not been investigated in pediatric subjects.
Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
Data:
There were no reports of overdose with ziftomenib treatment. No studies of drug abuse or
dependence were conducted with ziftomenib. Ziftomenib is not chemically or pharmacologically
similar to drugs with known abuse potential, and it does not produce known psychoactive effects.
Studies on withdrawal and rebound with ziftomenib were not conducted in this patient
population.
The Applicant’s Position:
Neither the effects of ziftomenib nor an antidote to overdose are known. No information is
available regarding withdrawal or rebound effects of ziftomenib.
Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable; ziftomenib has not been approved or marketed for commercial use in any
country.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
patients). As noted above, the all-grade rate of DS in KO-MEN-001 was higher in the population
with a KMT2A-r compared to the population with an NPM1-m (42% vs 24%, respectively).
Ultimately, the frequency and potential severity of DS with ziftomenib monotherapy use led the
Applicant to propose a boxed warning for DS. The FDA agreed with this recommendation and
ensured that the warning and precaution for DS described the trial experience for the labeled
patient population of adult patients with R/R AML with a susceptible NPM1-m, as well as the
higher rate and severity of DS in patients with KMT2A-r acute leukemia to fully inform treating
physicians (see Section 11).
QTc prolongation also occurred with ziftomenib treatment and was not caused by active AML.
The FDA analysis identified that the rate of any QT prolongation in the key safety population
was 12% (13/112). Moreover, 8% (9/112) of the key safety population had grade 3 events with
QTc prolongation of either > 60ms from baseline or a QTc > 500ms. There were no grade 4 or 5
events reported. The rate of QTc prolongation was lower in the overall KO-MEN-001 and ISS
populations at 8% all grade and 5% grade 3 in both. Due to the single arm nature of these
studies, it is difficult to account for the confounding effects of concomitant medications that are
also known to increase the QTc interval. However, the FDA analysis of ziftomenib concentration
versus ΔQTc combined with the potential for ziftomenib concentration boosting from drug-drug
interactions and food effects combined with the observed AE rate, led FDA to add a warning and
precautions for QTc prolongation to the ziftomenib label.
With these additional mitigation strategies, the FDA agrees with the Applicant that based on the
totality of efficacy and safety data, the benefit/risk profile of ziftomenib in the treatment of
patients with R/R AML with a susceptible NPM1 mutation who have no satisfactory alternative
treatment options is favorable.

256
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
participants receiving ziftomenib for > 6 months. However, the short-term benefits of ziftomenib
are considered to be meaningful.
Ziftomenib was generally well-tolerated, with toxicities that were manageable by dose
interruption. Serious risks include differentiation syndrome and QTc prolongation, which are
mitigated by appropriate labeling. Fatal adverse reactions included DS, infection, and sudden
death. However, the long-term safety of ziftomenib has not been established, and requires a
postmarketing study. In addition, the short duration of response, modest CR + CRh rate, and low
conversion to transfusion independence preclude the use of ziftomenib in the general relapsed or
refractory population, who would be better served by cytotoxic chemotherapy or agents targeting
other mutations. Therefore, the review team recommend approval for ziftomenib for the
treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory AML with an NPM1 mutation who have
no satisfactory alternative treatment options.

12 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
The FDA’s Assessment:
Based on this review, a REMS is not necessary to ensure that the benefits outweigh the risks of
ziftomenib for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory AML harboring a
susceptible NPM1 mutation who have no satisfactory alternative treatment options. The
management of the risks associated with ziftomenib treatment, including differentiation
syndrome and QTc prolongation, will be communicated through labeling.

leukemia cells with NPM1 mutations other than the A, B, and D subtypes.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
The FDA’s Assessment:
The PopPK model is adequate to describe the observed PK data of ziftomenib in adult patients
with R/R AML. There is no clinical meaningful impact for covariates including body weight,
sex, race, ethnicity, age (18-86 years old), AML mutation (NPM1-m, KMT2A-r or others), ALP,
ALT, AST, renal and hepatic impairment (mild or moderate), and p-gp inhibition. These
covariates were evaluated using forward addition & backward elimination and dropped out from
PopPK model. The impact of severe renal or hepatic impairment were not evaluated due to lack
of data. Based on the model, typical value of CL an in subjects with co-administration of strong
CYP3A4 inhibitors were 54.1% lower than those in subjects without co-administration of
CYP3A4 inhibitors. Coadministration of PPI reduced the relative bioavailability (F1) and the
first rate of absorption (Ka); F1 and Ka of ziftomenib with PPI would be 37.3% and 38.4% lower
than F1 and Ka without PPI, respectively. Administration of ziftomenib in presence of food
increased F1 and delayed the absorption; F1 of ziftomenib with food would be ~6.1 times higher
than F1 under fasting and the lag time of absorption would be ~1.38 time longer than under
fasting condition.
For metabolites KO-516 and KO-739, the PopPK model was developed by sequential 2-stage
approach by using empirical Bayesian estimates derived from the final Ziftomenib PopPK model
(FDA Figure 5). A two-compartmental model with linear elimination for both metabolites were
retained for popPK model. The final popPK model estimates for metabolites are listed as below
(FDA Table 60). The GOF plots are listed in FDA Figure 6. The pcVPC plots are listed in FDA
Figure 7. The PopPK model is adequate to describe the observed PK data of Ziftomenib
metabolites KO-516 and KO-739 in adult patients with R/R AML.

14.4.3.5.

Overall benefit-risk evaluation based on E-R analyses

The Applicant’s Position:
The E-R analyses support the overall favorable benefit-risk for ziftomenib. E-R analyses support
the proposed dose of 600 mg QD, as it demonstrated robust efficacy in the indicated population,
with a manageable safety profile. Ziftomenib is to be administered in fasted state. PPI dosing
should be staggered with respect to ziftomenib administration and CYP3A4 inducers are to be
avoided. No other covariates were found to have significant or clinically relevant impact on
ziftomenib efficacy or safety. Hence no dose adjustment is needed. The proposed ziftomenib
dose of 600 mg QD met all requirements for an optimal dose and provides a positive benefit-risk
profile.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Based on the PopPK and ER analysis result, 600 mg QD dose administered at fasting state is
acceptable. No dose adjustment is necessary when co-administered ziftomenib with CYP3A
inhibitors. Please refer to the PBPK review for the appropriateness of staggered PPI dosing.
Physiologically based Pharmacokinetic Modeling
14.4.4.1.

Executive Summary

The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s physiologically based
pharmacokinetic (PBPK) analyses to:
•

•

evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of ziftomenib in patients with cancer
o as an object of Strong (itraconazole), moderate (fluconazole) and weak (cimetidine)
CYP3A inhibitors
o as an object of strong (rifampin), moderate (efavirenz) and weak (dexamethasone)
CYP3A inducers
o as an inhibitor of CYP3A and UGT1A1
evaluate the effect of staggered dosing of a proton pump inhibitor (PPI) on ziftomenib
exposure.

The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK submission (reports ko-adme-0201kurg1g-pbpk and ko-pk-0004 and related model summary reports, responses to FDA PBPK
information requests dated June 20, 2025 (seq0018), July 28, 2025 (seq0026), and July 31, 2025
(seq0024), and the modeling supporting files), to conclude the following:
303
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
•

•
•

The PBPK analyses are inadequate to predict the effects of CYP3A precipitants on
ziftomenib exposure in patients with cancer because the available PK data from the
patients cannot verify the fraction of ziftomenib metabolized by CYP3A. Therefore, drug
interactions of ziftomenib with CYP3A precipitants were estimated based on in vitro
phenotyping, human mass balance and absolute bioavailability data:
o The strong CYP3A inhibitors itraconazole and posaconazole were estimated to
increase ziftomenib exposure up to 3-fold.
o The moderate CYP3A inhibitors isavuconazole, erythromycin, and fluconazole
were estimated to increase ziftomenib exposure up to 2-fold.
o The strong CYP3A inducer rifampin was estimated to reduce ziftomenib exposure
by up to 80%.
o The moderate CYP3A inducer efavirenz was estimated to reduce ziftomenib
exposure by up to 70%.
o The weak CYP3A inducer dexamethasone was estimated to reduce ziftomenib
exposure by up to 50%.
o Low fraction absorbed (approximately 30%) and potentially greater than 30% of
the drug was excretion as unchanged parent indicate a potential role of P-gp and
BCRP in ziftomenib absorption and excretion.
Ziftomenib is expected to have a weak-to-moderate inhibitory effect on the sensitive
CYP3A substrate midazolam and have minimal effects on the UGT1A1 substrate
raltegravir and substrates of CYP2C isoforms.
The PBPK analyses do not support staggered dosing of PPIs to help minimize the
interactions between PPIs and ziftomenib following multiple-dose administration.
14.4.4.2.

Background

Ziftomenib (also known as KO-539) is a menin-MLL (KMT2A) inhibitor, which is being
developed for the use as monotherapy in the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or
refractory acute lymphoblastic leukemia (R/R AML) with a nucleophosmin 1 (NPM1) mutation.
The proposed dosing regimens of ziftomenib is 600 mg once daily, taken fasted with water at
least 1 hour before or 2 hours after a meal. The available dose strength is 200 mg capsule.
Following oral administration of a single dose of 400 mg ziftomenib in healthy subjects under
the fasted state, a median time to peak concentration (tmax) was approximately 3 hours (KOMEN-003). Ziftomenib increased more than dose proportionally over the single-dose range of 50
mg to 1000 mg (Cycle 1 Day1 in part 1a) and 200 mg to 800 mg once daily (Cycle 2 Day 1 in
phase 1b) in patients with R/R AML (KO-MEN-001). High inter-patient coefficient of variation
(CV)% was observed ranging from 89% to 191% in part 1a and from 89% to 243% in part 1b.
Steady state was reached within 15 days. Administration of ziftomenib once daily resulted in
approximately 5-fold accumulation in AUC0-12 at steady state. At the 600 mg dose, the mean
steady-state ziftomenib Cmax was 800 ng/mL and AUC0-24 was 12200 ng/mL (KO-PK-0001).
Compared to the fasted state, the median tmax was delayed 2 hours, AUC and Cmax increased
304
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
by approximately 4-fold in healthy adults following a single 400 mg ziftomenib dose after a
high-fat meal in healthy subjects (KO-MEN-003). Coadministration of a single dose of 20 mg
rabeprazole, a gastric acid-reducing agent and proton pump inhibitor (PPI), with a single 400 mg
ziftomenib in healthy subjects under the fasted state, ziftomenib AUC0-inf and Cmax reduced
53% and 70%, respectively (KO-MEN-003). Steady state ziftomenib Ctrough, Cmax and AUC
were 3.2-, 2.1 and 2.5-fold higher in patients compared to healthy subjects (KO-PK-0001).
Ziftomenib is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 in vitro (KO-ADME-0050 and KO-ADME0056). The human ADME study in healthy male adult subjects (KO-MEN-005) following a
single oral dose of 400 mg ziftomenib showed that approximately 90.3% of the dose
administered orally was recovered, in which approximately 89.7% and 0.53% of the dose were
recovered in the feces and the urine, respectively. Ziftomenib accounted for 73.3% of the dose in
the feces, whereas 0.028% of the dose was excreted as unchanged drug in the urine, indicating
that renal elimination of ziftomenib was minimal. Ziftomenib and its metabolite KO-739
accounted for 15.1% and 9.2% of the total radioactivity exposure in plasma, respectively, and the
AUC0-inf of KO-739 was approximately 21% of its parent AUC (KO-MEN-005). Because the
metabolite to parent AUC ratio is less than 25%, there is no need to consider the DDI potential of
KO-739 (ICH guideline M12). The absolute bioavailability study, conducted along with the
human ADME study using an intravenous (IV) microdose of ziftomenib (100 µg), showed that
ziftomenib had an oral bioavailability of 12.9%, total plasma clearance of 30.3 L/h, and a renal
clearance of 0.0867 L/h (KO-MEN-005). Half-life was 25.1 hour following IV administration of
a single 100 mg ziftomenib and 53.5 hours following oral administration of a single 400 mg
ziftomenib.
Based on in vitro drug interaction studies, ziftomenib is a time-dependent and reversible
inhibitors of CYP3A4 (KO-ADME-0060, -0062 and -0065), and a reversible inhibitor of
ziftomenib of UGT1A1, UGT1A9 and UGT2B17 with IC50 values of 3.4, 1.4 and 1.1 µM,
respectively (KO_ADME-0061). Ziftomenib increased CYP1A2 mRNA expression in all three
donors in a concentration-dependent manner, but not CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 (KO-ADME-0059).
Ziftomenib is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP, but not a substrate of MRP2 and BSEP (KOADME-0038). Ziftomenib is not an inhibitor of any of the transporters studied (KO-ADME0038). Refer to the Clinical Pharmacology review section for detail information on ziftomenib
regarding ADME properties, in vitro and clinical studies used in the PBPK modeling analyses.
14.4.4.3.

Methods

All simulations were performed using the PK/PD Profiles mode in the Simcyp® Simulator
(Version 23, Certara, Sheffield, UK) unless noted below. Simulations were performed using the
default cancer population (Sim-Cancer) model to represent adult patients with cancer and the
default healthy subject population model (Sim-Healthy Volunteers) to represent healthy subjects.
Schemas of the PBPK modeling and simulation strategy are shown in FDA Figure 10, which
summarizes the studies used for model development and verification, and model applications of
ziftomenib as an object or precipitant of enzyme induction or inhibition.
305
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
AUC (2.3-fold) and Cmax (2.0-fold) between patients who were taking ziftomenib on Cycle 1
Day 1 (C1D1) with and without co-administration of a strong CYP3A inhibitor (posaconazole or
voriconazole). Additional sensitivity analysis determined that the fm,CYP3A value of 0.6 might be a
more conservative estimate compared to the value of 0.5 though both could reproduce the
calculated effects of strong CYP3A inhibitors. The model with the fm,CYP3A value of 0.6
predicted that a moderate CYP3A inhibitor such as fluconazole increased ziftomenib AUC and
Cmax 1.6- and 1.3-fold, respectively, whereas the calculated effects were 1.1-fold for AUC and
1.2-fold for Cmax. As discussed in the Results section 1, the available PK data from the patients
could not be used to adequately verify the fm,CYP3A value of ziftomenib.
Using data from in vitro reaction phenotyping, human mass balance and absolute bioavailability
studies, in combination with Sensitivity Analysis, the reviewer estimated that an fm,CYP3A of 0.7
could be the worst case scenario: based on the metabolism studies conducted using recombinant
CYP enzymes and isoform-selective chemical inhibitors with human liver microsomes (KOADME-0056), approximately 88-96% of ziftomenib metabolism was mediated by CYP3A4. In
the human ADME study (KO-MEN-005), assuming 100% recovery of radioactivity,
approximately 81% of the administered dose was identified as unchanged parent, and
metabolites accounted for approximately 19% of the doses. The absolute bioavailability (BA)
study (KO-MEN-005) determined the plasma clearance of ziftomenib (30.3 L/h) and the product
(0.27) of intestinal availability (Fg) and fraction absorbed (fa). Assuming low to no first-pass
metabolism (Fg ~0.8 to 1), fa was estimated to be 0.27 to 0.33, then the fraction metabolized (fm)
was estimated to be 0.56 to 0.70. Assuming that all the oxidative metabolites were properly
identified in the human ADME study, the fm,CYP3A value of ziftomenib would range from 0.49 to
0.67, which is close to the optimized value discussed earlier. For evaluation of the potential
worst-case scenario of DDIs, the Applicant was requested to simulate the effects of CYP3A
inhibitors and inducers assuming a ziftomenib fm,CYP3A value of 0.7. The results are presented in
FDA Table 66.
The Reviewer acknowledges, however, that there are several limitations of these estimated
results: (1) in vitro, ziftomenib was determined to be a substrate of P-gp and BCRP and has lowto-moderate passive permeability (1.1 x10-6 cm/s). In the BA study, as mentioned above, no
more than 70% of the absorbed drug was metabolized, thus at least 30% of the drug was excreted
as unchanged parent via the intestinal/ biliary route. Together with the low fraction absorbed of
ziftomenib, these data indicate that we cannot dismiss a potential role of P-gp and BCRP in
ziftomenib absorption and excretion, which was not considered in the estimated effects. (2)
Population PK analysis showed that ziftomenib clearance in patients with cancer was 2.6-fold
lower compared to healthy subjects. The mechanisms for this difference are unclear, thus this
clearance difference was not considered in the estimation. The default cancer population model
assumes that there is no difference in CYP3A abundance compared to the healthy population
model. If the low clearance in the patient population was due to low CYP3A abundance in
patients with cancer, then the potential effects of CYP3A inhibition would be overestimated. It
should be noted that, for strong CYP3A inhibitors, the estimated effects were generally
consistent with the population PK analysis, which showed the effects of strong CYP3A inhibitors
313
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5694243

NDA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220305
Komzifti (ziftomenib)
•

•

Following administration of 600 mg ziftomenib QD, the steady state ziftomenib AUC and
Cmax in patients are 12200 h*ng/mL and 881 ng/mL (proposed ziftomenib label). However,
the exposure of ziftomenib at the last dose in the simulated midazolam DDI study (simulation
output file ‘ir-06202025-38.xlsx’) was 913 h*ng/mL for AUC and 124 ng/mL for Cmax,
which are more than 13- and 7-fold lower than those estimated from patients, respectively.
The Applicant argued that the steady-state exposure of ziftomenib was calculated based on
PK data from patients who were on posaconazole (strong CYP3A inhibitor) and should not
be used to evaluate the effect of ziftomenib on midazolam exposure (Response to FDA Clin
Pharm IR dated June 20, 2025, seq 0018). The reviewer disagrees. In the Phase 1/2 study
(KO-MEN-001), 82% of cancer patients with NPM1-m were co-administered ziftomenib
with strong CYP3A inhibitors, and the descriptive statistics of the steady-state exposure
parameters of 600 mg ziftomenib in this population (N=94 out of 100 efficacy population and
N=111 out of 119 safety population) were similar to the values in the proposed label (Table
11 and Table 12 in report KO-PK-0001). Therefore, the Applicant was requested to simulate
the effect of ziftomenib on substrates of CYP3A, CYP2C isoenzymes, and UGT1A1 using
the steady-state exposure of ziftomenib, and the results are presented in FDA Table 67. Of
note, the population PK analysis estimated for steady state (C2D1) ziftomenib AUC and Cmax
in patients not receiving azoles were 5512 h*ng/ml and 288 ng/mL, which are also greater
than the original Applicant’s simulated exposure (reviewer’s analysis). During the labeling
negotiation, the Applicant performed an addition simulation and found that, at a ziftomenib
AUC of 6220 *ng/ml, ziftomenib was predicted to increase the AUC and Cmax of midazolam
by up to 1.9- and 1.4-fold.
The Applicant provided predictions of the effect of ziftomenib on the CYP3A substrate
venetoclax. However, these predictions are considered exploratory (data not shown) since no
documentation of venetoclax model development and verification was provided by the
Applicant (Response to FDA Clin Pharm IR dated June 20, 2025, seq 0018).
14.4.4.4.4.
Effects of staggered dosing of proton pump inhibitors on the
exposure of ziftomenib

The Applicant proposed staggering dosing of a proton pump inhibitor (PPI) (administered 2-12 h
after ziftomenib) to minimize its effect on decreasing ziftomenib exposure, as observed with
rabeprazole (KO-MEN-003).
The PBPK analyses indicate that staggered dosing would not help minimize the interaction
between PPIs (e.g., rabeprazole and esomeprazole) and ziftomenib for the following reasons:
•

As illustrated in FDA Figure 13, at the Applicant’s proposed ziftomenib administration
time points, the gastric pH values (intersections of the blue vertical lines with the
observed time courses of gastric pH on Day 7), are mostly above the pH value at which
the effect of rabeprazole on ziftomenib was observed (intersection of the red vertical and
horizontal lines). Therefore, PPIs are expected to have the same, if not greater, effects on
ziftomenib exposure as that observed if ziftomenib is administered at these time points.
